@@l0@@
Part 0

The question, and three answers nobody else will write

"What do you hope to learn whilst interning at KKR?" 1,000 characters. Start here, then read the modules to own every word.

What the question is really testing@@l0-read@@

A learning question is a disguised knowledge question. Most applicants answer it with soft goals: "exposure to a world-class firm", "develop my communication skills", "learn about private markets". Those sentences tell the reader nothing they can check. A strong answer names specific technical things that only exist inside this seat, shows you already understand enough to know what you do not know, and ties each item to KKR rather than to the industry INFERRED.

Specific
Name a fund, a vehicle, a mechanism. "K-INFRA's quarterly 5% repurchase limit" beats "how evergreen funds work".
Only here
Tie it to something KKR's structure creates: one P&L, KCS with Capital Markets, flagship and K-Series sharing deals.
Honest
Say "learn", not "contribute expertise". Show curiosity with enough precision to prove you did the work.

Three answers, each under 1,000 characters@@l0-answers@@

All three are written from facts in this guide. Character counts include spaces and were checked by script. Pick one, then make it yours: change at least a few words so it sounds like you, and re-count after editing. If the form counts line breaks, keep it as one paragraph.

Version A: the technical spine (recommended) · 947 characters including spaces

I hope to learn how one client team turns the whole of KKR into the right answer for each client. First, the plumbing: why the same infrastructure team reaches a pension through a closed-end fund like GII V, but a private bank client through K-INFRA, with monthly NAV, a performance fee over a hurdle and redemptions capped at 5% a quarter, and what this year's private credit redemption queues taught the industry about setting expectations. Second, how clients decide: commitment pacing, DPI against IRR, and how KKR's Total Portfolio Approach turns "buy fund X" into "build a private markets programme". Third, what only KKR's structure shows: how KCS and Capital Markets syndicate co-investment to clients, and how information walls hold while it happens. Under one P&L, the team can recommend what fits the client rather than what one fund needs. I want to leave able to explain any KKR product precisely, because at KKR trust is the product.

Version B: the London and private wealth angle · 889 characters including spaces

In London, I hope to learn how private markets are opened to new investors without breaking promises made to existing ones. KKR's K-Series has grown to $42bn, and in Europe K-PRIME must work within AIFMD II's liquidity rules and the FCA's Consumer Duty, while UK pensions move towards private assets under the Pension Schemes Act. I want to understand the technical side: how an evergreen vehicle shares deals with KKR's flagship funds, how NAV, fees and quarterly redemption limits are set, and how a private bank's due diligence team tests all of it. I also want to learn the institutional craft: answering DDQs accurately, building client materials, and seeing how KCS works with Capital Markets on co-investment. Most of all, I want to learn to explain a complex product to someone trusting it with other people's money, which Henry Kravis's "like and trust" puts at the centre of KKR.

Version C: personal hook, then technical (fill the brackets; about 260 characters free) · 734 characters including spaces

When I [your example: explained X to Y / kept Z running under pressure], I learned that [one-line lesson]. KCS is where that becomes the whole job, and I want to learn its technical core. First, how KKR packages one strategy for different clients: infrastructure through GII V for a pension, or through K-INFRA, with monthly NAV and quarterly redemption limits, for a private bank client. Second, how clients think: commitment pacing, DPI versus IRR, and KKR's Total Portfolio Approach. Third, how co-investment moves from Capital Markets to KCS clients behind information walls, which KKR's one-P&L structure makes possible. I want to leave able to explain any KKR product accurately to someone trusting it with other people's money.

Anatomy of Version A: where every claim comes from@@l0-anatomy@@

Every phrase in Version A is something you can defend for twenty minutes in an interview. This table maps each one to its evidence and to the module in this guide that teaches it.

Phrase in the answerThe fact behind itLearn it in

Don't write this
  • A K-PEC or K-INFRA minimum investment: not in the filings read.
  • That KKR has an ELTIF or a UK LTAF: no evidence was found.
  • A KKR "40/30/30" portfolio: KKR publishes no such model; its wealth model portfolios work out at about 45/25/30 and 30/40/30 stocks, bonds and private assets [S435, S436].
  • "KKR is the largest" or "leads private wealth": Blackstone and Apollo are larger, and Blackstone leads wealth [S213, S215].
  • Anything you cannot explain if asked "what do you mean by that?"

How the guide is built@@l0-map@@

How to use this guide in one evening

Read Part 0 and Part 7 first (30 minutes). Pick your answer version. Then read only the modules behind the phrases you kept, so you can survive three follow-up questions on each. The rest is for the interview.

@@l1@@
Part 1

What KCS actually does, as a set of technical problems

Forget the job title. A KCS desk is where three hard problems meet: what the client can own, what the product can promise, and what the firm can give. Learning to solve all three at once is the technical skill this seat teaches.

One seat, three problems@@l1-seat@@

Most descriptions of client solutions sound like sales. The technical reality is closer to engineering. Every time a client gives KKR money, three sets of constraints have to fit together:

  • The client's problem. A pension fund, a sovereign wealth fund, an insurer or a wealthy family each has bills to pay (liabilities), a return it needs, a date it needs cash back, rules about what it may own, and a speed at which it can commit new money.
  • The product's problem. Each KKR product has a legal wrapper, a liquidity promise, a fee formula, a home country and a size. These cannot be changed for one client without affecting every other client in the same vehicle.
  • The firm's problem. KKR has finite deal capacity, many clients who want the same deal, legal walls around inside information, and one shared profit pool that rewards selling the right thing over selling anything.

KKR's own job text frames the seat exactly this way. KCS "seeks to 'connect the dots' across all businesses at KKR, from our Private Equity investing teams to Infrastructure, Real Estate, Credit, Capital Markets and Insurance-Funded Strategies" [S141] CONFIRMED. It is "primarily organized around two specialized focus areas: Sales/Relationship Management and Product/Strategy Management" [S141] CONFIRMED. Relationship managers own the client's problem. Product strategists own the product's problem. The firm's problem is owned by everyone, which is why a junior who understands it stands out INFERRED.

What you will learn

To read any investment request as a constraint problem with three parts, and to say which part is binding. That is the habit that turns "sales support" into structured problem solving.

Where you meet it at KKR

In the London analyst duties: "Collaborating with various internal divisions globally to answer client requests (Product teams, Investment teams, Operations, Finance, Legal, Compliance, Risk)" [S101] CONFIRMED. Each division in that list owns a piece of one of the three problems INFERRED.

Only at KKR

KKR runs "1 firm with 1 compensation approach" (CFO Robert Lewin) [S207] CONFIRMED, and its asset management costs sit in "a single expense pool" [S70] CONFIRMED. So the firm's problem is not someone else's P&L to argue with: it is the same pool the client team is paid from. That changes what a junior is taught to optimise INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn how KCS turns a client's liabilities, a product's liquidity terms and the firm's capacity into one answer, because that is where the technical work in fundraising really sits."

Problem one: the client's problem@@l1-client@@

Every institutional client can be described by five numbers or rules. Learn these five and you can read any client in minutes.

ConstraintPlain EnglishThe technical question
LiabilitiesThe money the client owes in future: pensions to retirees, insurance claims, a government's future spending.How long until the money is needed (duration), and is it fixed or linked to inflation?
Return targetThe return needed to meet those liabilities.Which assets can reach it after fees, and with how much risk?
LiquidityHow much cash must be available, and when.What share of the portfolio can be locked up for 10+ years?
RegulationThe rules the client itself lives under.Can it legally hold this asset, in this vehicle, from this country?
PacingThe speed at which it can sign new commitments.How much must it commit each year to reach a target allocation, given that drawdown funds call money slowly?

The six client types, constraint by constraint

Defined benefit (DB) pension. A DB scheme promises a fixed pension, so its liabilities are known and shrink as members retire. KKR's institutional page offers corporate pensions "consultative advice to corporations who are considering the benefits and challenges associated with de-risking their defined benefit plans" [S81] CONFIRMED. Its signature technical trap is the denominator effect: listed shares are repriced daily, private assets only quarterly, so a market fall makes the private share look too big and freezes new commitments [S323] REPORTED. S&P Global found 174 of 298 pension funds over-allocated to private equity in Q1 2025 [S324] REPORTED. Bain cites an ILPA survey in which 53% of LPs said undrawn commitments limit new private equity commitments [S262] CONFIRMED.

Worked example: the denominator effect (illustrative numbers)

A £10bn pension targets 15% in private markets: £1.5bn private, £8.5bn listed. Listed markets fall 20%: £8.5bn × 0.8 = £6.8bn. Private marks lag and stay at £1.5bn. New total £8.3bn. Private share = 1.5 ÷ 8.3 = 18.1%, above the 15% target, although the scheme bought nothing.

Formula in words: private share = private value ÷ (private value + listed value). Only the denominator moved. The scheme's committee now pauses commitments, and KKR's next fundraise meets a "not this year". A KCS junior who can run this sum for a client's published asset mix can predict which clients will be quiet before the relationship manager asks INFERRED.

UK defined contribution (DC) megafund. DC savers own their pots, the default fund is priced and dealt in daily, and fees are scrutinised INFERRED. The Pension Schemes Act 2026 (Royal Assent 29 April 2026) requires large multi-employer DC schemes to have a default fund of at least £25bn by 2030, with a transition route for schemes of £10bn that can reach £25bn by 2035 [S346] CONFIRMED. It also gives the government a reserve power, usable once and not before 1 January 2028, to require up to 10% of the default fund in "qualifying assets" (private equity, private credit, infrastructure, land, unlisted shares), of which no more than 5% UK-specific; trustees can opt out if compliance is "likely not to be in the best interests of members" [S346] CONFIRMED. This mirrors the voluntary Mansion House Accord of 13 May 2025: 17 providers covering about 90% of active DC savers pledged 10% of default funds to private markets by 2030, at least 5% in the UK [S315] CONFIRMED.

Gulf sovereign wealth fund. Generational horizon, little need for cash, very large tickets. Global SWF put Gulf sovereign assets at about $5.7tn and found they "have shown no sign of slowdown (yet)" through the 2026 Iran war, apart from Qatar's QIA, which slowed [S365] REPORTED. KKR's 10-K warns that sovereign funds and public pensions show "an increased preference for alternatives to traditional fund structures, such as separately managed accounts" and are "consolidating their capital with fewer alternative asset managers" [S70] CONFIRMED. The binding constraint is not liquidity: it is getting enough size, at low enough fees, with enough control INFERRED.

Life insurer. An insurer invests policyholders' premiums to pay future claims, so its assets must match the timing of its liabilities (asset-liability management, ALM) and it holds regulatory capital against every asset. KKR's insurance client team offers "fully integrated balance sheet solutions that combine KKR's leading asset origination capabilities, deep capital markets access ... liability structuring solutions, and expert insights into global regulatory frameworks, asset allocation, portfolio construction, and ALM" [S104] CONFIRMED. An insurance product strategist role requires knowledge of "RBC, Solvency II" (the US and EU insurance capital regimes) [S105] CONFIRMED. Third-party insurers are 11% of KKR's AUM and Global Atlantic's own balance sheet another 22% [S71] CONFIRMED.

Family office. A private investment company for one wealthy family. Light regulation, long horizon, often a preference for deal-by-deal choice. KKR's family capital page lists "Co-Investment Opportunities", "Customized Portfolio Solutions", "Bespoke Separately Managed Accounts", "Two-Way Sourcing Dialogues" and "Comprehensive Debt Solutions" [S84] CONFIRMED. The last two are unusual: the family is treated as a deal partner and a borrower as well as an investor INFERRED.

Private bank client. An individual investing through a bank. The binding constraint is suitability: the distributor must check the product fits the client. KKR's 10-K says MiFID II, AIFMD and the ELTIF Regulation "contain requirements for, among other things, marketing, investor suitability assessments, and conflicts of interest management" that also apply to "distributors, placement agents and other intermediaries" [S70] CONFIRMED. In the UK, the high-net-worth exemption for financial promotions uses £100,000 income or £250,000 net assets (restored on 27 March 2024) [S307] CONFIRMED. In the US, K-Series is built for accredited investors, "$1 million and up in net worth" (Scott Nuttall) [S76] REPORTED.

What you will learn

To profile any client on five constraints, compute the denominator effect and a commitment pacing gap, and identify the single binding constraint before anyone opens a pitch book.

Where you meet it at KKR

KKR's investor base spans "65 countries" and runs from pensions to sovereigns, insurers, endowments and individuals [S70] CONFIRMED. The Singapore KCS intern brief includes "conducting market and client research" and "preparing client briefing materials" [S107] CONFIRMED: the five-constraint profile is that briefing, done properly INFERRED.

Only at KKR

Private wealth (18% of AUM) is now larger than pensions (14%), and insurance in total is about a third [S71] CONFIRMED; the one-third sum is INFERRED. A London junior sees all six rows of the grid in one team, not one or two. A single-strategy firm's IR team typically meets mainly the top three rows INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn to read a client the way KKR's insurance team describes it, through liabilities, capital rules and ALM, and then do the same for a pension, a sovereign and a private bank client."

Problem two: the product's problem@@l1-product@@

A KKR product is a set of five design dials. Turning one dial for one client changes the deal for everyone else in the vehicle, which is why "customised" usually means a different vehicle, not a different term INFERRED.

DialOptions on KKR's shelf, with sourced examples
StructureClosed-end drawdown fund (North America Fund XIV, Global Infrastructure Investors V); separately managed account (SMA, a fund of one); co-investment in a single deal; strategic multi-asset partnership; insurance sidecar (Global Atlantic's Ivy vehicles, $62bn) [S70, S84, S85, S200] CONFIRMED.
LiquidityDrawdown funds: none until exits. Infrastructure and real estate drawdown funds run 10 to 13 years; credit 8 to 10 (up to 12); core private equity up to 25 years; open-ended vehicles have no end date [S2] CONFIRMED. Evergreen funds: typically up to 5% of the fund per quarter [S278] REPORTED. Capital Group KKR interval funds: repurchase offers of up to 10% of shares quarterly at NAV [S92] CONFIRMED.
FeesDrawdown private equity: 1.0% to 2.0% of committed capital in the investment period, then 0.75% to 1.50% of invested capital. Drawdown infrastructure and real estate: 0.75% to 1.50%. Open-ended vehicles including K-Series: 0.50% to 1.25%. Carry: 10% to 20% of profits after a 6% to 8% hurdle [S2] CONFIRMED. K-PEC and K-INFRA main share classes: 1.25% a year of NAV [S111, S112] CONFIRMED. Capital Group KKR Core Plus+: 0.84% total expense ratio [S92] CONFIRMED.
VehicleK-PEC is a US LLC; K-PRIME is a Luxembourg SICAV and FCP, the non-US twin "with similar objectives and strategies"; K-FIT is a US business development company (BDC) [S111, S113] CONFIRMED.
JurisdictionKKR's EU AIFM (the regulated manager) is KKR Alternative Investment Management Unlimited Company, authorised in Ireland; outside the US KKR relies on "private placement exemptions and marketing registrations" under AIFMD in Europe and the UK's FSMA and AIFM Regulations [S70] CONFIRMED.
Worked example: same strategy, two wrappers, two fee bills (illustrative numbers)

Drawdown fund. A client commits $100m to a private equity fund charging 1.5% on commitments in the investment period (inside KKR's 1.0% to 2.0% range [S2]). Year 1 fee = $100m × 1.5% = $1.5m, even if only $20m has been called. Fee as a share of money actually invested = 1.5 ÷ 20 = 7.5% in year 1, falling as capital is called.

Evergreen fund. The same client buys $100m of an evergreen PE vehicle at 1.25% of NAV (K-PEC's main classes [S111]). The money is invested at once, so the fee is $1.25m on $100m working from day one.

Lesson: "1.5% versus 1.25%" is the wrong comparison. The right one is fee per dollar actually invested, over time, plus the cost of holding cash while waiting for capital calls. A KCS junior who can show that table to a private bank's product committee is doing product strategy, not admin INFERRED.

What you will learn

The five dials of a fund product, how each changes cash flows and fees, and why a liquidity promise must be matched by the assets inside. You learn to price a product on the client's cash flows, not its headline rate.

Where you meet it at KKR

Matt Yates in London is "Head of EMEA Product Development" and runs product management for K-PRIME and K-PEC [S133] CONFIRMED. Amy Lesch's global product team covers "product origination and management across private equity, infrastructure, real estate, insurance, and credit" [S121] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

One strategy can be sold through every column of the vehicle map. In Q2 2026 real assets capital came from "Helix Digital Infrastructure, K-Series Infrastructure, Asia Infrastructure III and Global Infrastructure Investors V" [S74] CONFIRMED: platform, evergreen and drawdown wrappers on the same asset class in one quarter. Blackstone also runs infrastructure in several wrappers (BXINFRA) [S213], so the claim is about seeing all wrappers plus an owned insurer and KCM from one desk, not about wrappers alone INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn why the same infrastructure strategy is sold as GII V, as K-Series Infrastructure and as a platform like Helix, and how the wrapper changes fees, liquidity and who can buy it."

Problem three: the firm's problem@@l1-firm@@

The client wants the best deal; the product has fixed terms; the firm has to decide what it can offer at all. Four technical issues sit here.

  1. Capacity. A fund has a size and an investment period. North America Fund XIV shows $21.9bn committed, investing April 2025 to April 2031; GII V $18.6bn, July 2024 to July 2030 [S1] CONFIRMED. Co-investment capacity depends on deal flow, not on demand.
  2. Allocation. When ten clients want one co-investment, someone decides the split. KKR Capital Markets "provides syndication services for co-investments in transactions participated in by KKR, our investment vehicles, Global Atlantic, and third-party clients" [S70] CONFIRMED. If clients do not take it up, "we may be required to fund the remaining commitment" [S70] CONFIRMED. Allocation errors are costly: in 2015 the SEC found KKR had misallocated more than $17m of broken-deal expenses to its flagship funds rather than to co-investors, and KKR paid nearly $30m [S20] CONFIRMED.
  3. Information walls. KCM handles financing and securities, so people can be "wall-crossed" onto live deals and must not use or pass on inside information INFERRED. Amy Lesch sits on KKR's "Global Conflicts and Compliance Committee" [S121] CONFIRMED.
  4. Most-favoured-nation (MFN) terms. Side letters give large LPs extra rights; an MFN clause lets other LPs claim terms given to similar or smaller LPs INFERRED. One concession can therefore spread across a whole fund.
What you will learn

How capacity, allocation policy, inside-information walls and MFN clauses limit what a client can be offered, and why fair allocation is a legal duty, not a courtesy.

Where you meet it at KKR

The KCS mandate says "Work in partnership with Capital Markets in the syndication/distribution of product" (KCS team description, brief). In Europe, Mark Danzey is both "European Head of KKR's Capital Markets team" and "European Head of Institutional Client Solutions" [S156] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

Blackstone (BXCM) and Apollo (Apollo Capital Solutions) also own capital markets arms [S214, S220] CONFIRMED, so the capability is not unique. What is unusual is that KKR writes the KCM link into the client team's own description and gives one European leader both hats [S156] CONFIRMED. The comparison is INFERRED. A junior here sees allocation from both sides of the wall's procedures.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn how KCS and Capital Markets decide who gets a co-investment, because that is where client service, capacity and compliance meet in one decision."

The matching engine@@l1-engine@@

Put the three problems together and the KCS job becomes a repeatable procedure. Think of it as an engine with four gates. Hard constraints come first because they are yes or no; soft preferences come last because they are trade-offs INFERRED.

  1. Can they own it? Rules and jurisdiction: client classification, marketing permissions, vehicle type.
  2. Can they hold it? Liquidity and horizon: lock-up versus the client's cash needs.
  3. Can they size it? Ticket, minimums, pacing and KKR's capacity.
  4. Should they want it? Net return, fees, fit with the rest of the portfolio, the house view.

In symbols: Solution = best by score ( Shelf ∩ Allowed ∩ Holdable ∩ Sizeable ). The ∩ means "products that pass every gate". KKR's macro team argues for scoring the whole portfolio, not one sleeve: its Total Portfolio Approach is "a governance and investment framework that seeks to improve capital allocation by allowing investors to evaluate opportunities across the total portfolio rather than through static asset class silos" [S90] CONFIRMED.

What you will learn

A disciplined order of questions: eligibility, then liquidity, then size, then preference. It stops the classic junior mistake of pitching the best-performing fund to a client who cannot legally or practically hold it.

Where you meet it at KKR

KKR's 10-K lists what fundraising competition turns on: "investment performance, investor liquidity and willingness to invest, investor reputation ... duration of relationships, quality of services, and pricing and fund terms, including fees" [S70] CONFIRMED. That list is the engine's scoring sheet.

Only at KKR

Most firms' engines have a short shelf. KCS supported 24 strategies by 2020, up from 4 in 2008 [S94] REPORTED, and its shelf now adds K-Series, Capital Group vehicles, Ivy sidecars and KCM financing. The longer the shelf, the more the gates matter, and the more a junior learns from running them INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn how KCS filters a 20-plus strategy shelf down to the two products a client can actually own, hold and size."

Three solutions, built step by step@@l1-cases@@

Three archetypal clients follow. They are illustrative composites, not real KKR clients. Every fact about KKR products, markets and rules is sourced; every client number is invented for the exercise and labelled.

Archetype A. A Gulf sovereign wanting a strategic partnership plus co-investment

Client (illustrative)A Gulf sovereign fund planning a $2.0bn programme with KKR over three years. Wants fewer, deeper manager relationships, lower blended fees, direct deal exposure, and themes tied to national priorities.

What the junior doesBuilds the blended fee table below; keeps the co-investment pipeline log and confidentiality tracker; prepares the meeting brief on the client's published themes; drafts the quarterly partnership report INFERRED, based on the duties in [S101, S107].

The formula: blended fee = Σ (size of sleeve × fee of sleeve) ÷ total size = 17.4 ÷ 2,000 = 0.87%. The trade-off KCS manages is that every fee point given away on the SMA is earned back only if the sovereign stays for the next vintages INFERRED.

Only at KKR

KKR can wrap fund, SMA, co-investment, a platform like Helix and a reinsurance sidecar into one relationship, with one European leader spanning KCM and institutional clients [S156, S200] CONFIRMED. It has localised coverage too: an Abu Dhabi office opened in November 2025 [S184] CONFIRMED and two Middle East KCS leaders were made MD from 1 January 2026 [S165] CONFIRMED.

Client (illustrative)A master trust with a £30bn default fund, 2% in private markets today, committed under the Mansion House Accord to 10% by 2030 with at least 5% in the UK. The Accord itself is sourced [S315] CONFIRMED; the scheme is invented.

What the junior doesBuilds the glidepath below; maps which KKR assets count as "UK"; drafts the RFP or tender answers with the CAPS team [S106, S146]; prepares consultant materials for EMEA consultant relations (Daniel Valladares) [S132] CONFIRMED.
Worked example: the pacing gap (illustrative numbers)

Target private NAV in 2030 = 10% × £30bn = £3.0bn. Today = 2% × £30bn = £0.6bn. Gap = £2.4bn. If a drawdown fund has invested only about half of a commitment after two years, the scheme must commit roughly £2.4bn ÷ 0.5 ≈ £4.8bn by 2028 to be invested by 2030. An evergreen or LTAF route invests on day one, so the commitment equals the target. That arithmetic, not fashion, is why DC schemes ask for evergreen wrappers INFERRED.

Only at KKR

KKR already designs a DC product elsewhere: with Capital Group it announced a target-date fund for US 401(k) plans in December 2025 [S363] REPORTED. A London junior can study how a US DC product was built while the UK market forms. Note the honest limit: no KKR UK DC mandate or LTAF was found in public sources [Gap].

To build a solution end to end: profile, binding constraint, product fit, blended fees, pacing, and the distribution gates. Three archetypes cover most of what a London desk sees.

@@l2@@
Part 2

Module 1: The plumbing of private markets products

Every KKR product is a pipe that carries money from a client to a deal and back. This module takes the pipes apart: who can use each one, when money moves, who gets paid in what order, and what happens when too many people want their money back at once.

Part 1 showed that a KCS desk matches a client's constraints to a product's terms. This part is the product side in full mechanical detail. By the end you should be able to take any KKR vehicle, say what legal box it sits in, draw how cash flows through it, compute its fees for a year, and explain what happens in a bad quarter. That is the vocabulary every product specialist, relationship manager and analyst in KCS uses every day INFERRED.

1. The vehicle menu: eleven ways to own the same kind of asset@@l2-menu@@

A vehicle (or "wrapper") is the legal container investors put money into. The assets inside can be similar: companies, power grids, loans, buildings. What changes from wrapper to wrapper is four things: who is allowed in, when cash moves, how fees are charged and how (and whether) you can get out. KKR's 10-K describes the range: drawdown private equity funds with management fees of 1.0% to 2.0% of committed capital during the investment period, drawdown infrastructure and real estate funds at 0.75% to 1.50%, and open-ended vehicles (core infrastructure, core+ real estate, evergreen direct lending, K-Series) at 0.50% to 1.25% [S2] CONFIRMED. Here is the menu, from most locked-up to most liquid.

WrapperWhat it is in one lineLive KKR example
Closed-end drawdown LP fundInvestors promise money; KKR calls it over years, invests, sells, and returns cash. Fixed life.Global Infrastructure Investors V (GII V), $19.2bn final close, 3 Aug 2026 [S430] CONFIRMED; North America Fund XIV, about $23bn [S335] CONFIRMED; European Fund VI, $7.5bn committed (about $8bn reported close) [S175, S185] CONFIRMED
Parallel fundA legally separate twin that invests side by side with the main fund, in fixed proportions, for investors who need a different legal or tax form.Generic structure INFERRED. CalPERS reports a holding in "KKR European Fund V (USD)" [S117] CONFIRMED, which suggests a currency-specific vehicle alongside the main fund INFERRED.
Feeder fundA pooling vehicle: many small investors buy into the feeder; the feeder makes one large commitment to the main fund."KKR FS Income Trust iCapital Offshore Access Fund SPC" on EDGAR [S114] CONFIRMED; K-PRIME's "Feeder-I" sub-fund investing into a master FCP [S429] REPORTED
Separately managed account (SMA)A "fund of one": a single large client with its own terms."Bespoke Separately Managed Accounts" for families [S84] CONFIRMED; ABF Partners II raised about $1bn of SMAs beside a $5.6bn fund [S433] REPORTED

How the institutional pieces fit around one deal

The top-left corner is not one product but a family. One KKR deal can be funded by five different pipes at once. The main fund and any parallel funds take their fixed shares. Feeders sit above the main fund and simply pass money through. SMAs invest next to the fund under their own contract. Co-investors buy extra pieces of that one deal. Since 2023 a sixth pipe exists: K-PEC (or K-INFRA in infrastructure) joins the deal as a co-general partner of a deal-level joint venture alongside the flagship fund [S420, S421] CONFIRMED.

Three wrappers that look odd until you see why they exist

Co-investment is a profit engine, not a giveaway. Since 2014 KKR's fund agreements "typically require us to share 100% of any monitoring, transaction, and break-up fees that are allocable to a fund" with fund investors, but "transaction fees earned in our capital markets business are not shared with fund investors" [S425] CONFIRMED. So when KCS places a slice of a deal with a co-investor, the co-investor pays a low fee, but the deal can generate KCM fees that fund investors do not offset [S425] INFERRED. KCM earned $930m of capital markets transaction fees in 2025 [S425] CONFIRMED. Industry-wide, PitchBook tracked $37.5bn of co-investment fundraising in the 12 months to June 2026 [S260] CONFIRMED.

SMAs are where the biggest clients are going. KKR's 10-K warns that sovereign wealth funds and public pension funds "continue to demonstrate an increased preference for alternatives to traditional fund structures, such as separately managed accounts" [S70] CONFIRMED.

Insurance sidecars turn equity into balance sheet. The Ivy vehicles are "commitment-based structures with third-party investors" that "participate alongside Global Atlantic through reinsurance" during their investment periods, and are supervised by the Bermuda Monetary Authority [S425] CONFIRMED. At Q2 2026 KKR described "$6 billion of dry powder that we think translates to north of $60 billion of buying power on the liability side" [S440] CONFIRMED. That is roughly $1 of investor equity supporting $10 of insurance liabilities [S440] INFERRED.

What you will learn

To classify any product by four questions (who may buy, when cash moves, how fees are charged, how you exit) and place it on the map in Figure 1. Once you can do that, a new product is a variation, not a mystery.

Where you meet it at KKR

A KKR Global Wealth Solutions posting asks for "depth of expertise across alternative assets classes in both private and registered structures (interval funds, BDCs, REITs)" [S103] CONFIRMED. In London, Matt Yates is "Head of EMEA Product Development" and runs product management for K-PRIME and K-PEC [S133] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

Most managers have drawdown funds, SMAs and an evergreen range. KKR adds two pipes few rivals combine with them: a capital markets desk whose co-investment fees sit outside the fund offset [S425], and an owned insurer whose sidecars take third-party equity [S425, S440]. Blackstone and Apollo also have in-house capital markets [S214, S220] CONFIRMED, so the honest claim is the combination on one shelf, not any single pipe INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn the whole KKR product shelf as one map, from GII V's drawdown fund to K-INFRA's monthly NAV, so I can explain why the same infrastructure asset sits in five different wrappers."

2. Drawdown mechanics: commitment, call, fee, return@@l2-drawdown@@

Step 1: the commitment

An institution signs the Limited Partnership Agreement (LPA, the fund's contract) and commits, say, $100m. No cash moves yet. The commitment is a legally binding promise to pay when asked INFERRED. KKR's 10-K describes this model: commit now, and KKR calls the money over the investment period [S70] CONFIRMED. Investment periods are long: North America Fund XIV invests from April 2025 to April 2031, GII V from July 2024 to July 2030, and European Fund VI from June 2022 to June 2028 [S1] CONFIRMED. Infrastructure and real estate drawdown funds have fund terms of 10 to 13 years [S2] CONFIRMED.

Step 2: the capital call

When KKR agrees a deal, the fund sends each LP a capital call notice (a drawdown notice): "please wire X by date Y". X is the LP's percentage of the fund times the cash needed. If a $10bn fund needs $400m for a deal plus $25m of fees and expenses, an LP with a $100m commitment (1% of the fund) wires $4.25m (illustrative numbers). The money not yet called is the uncalled commitment, also called "dry powder". KKR reported $142.7bn of uncalled commitments across the firm at 30 June 2026 [S1] CONFIRMED.

Key term

Uncalled commitment: commitment minus everything called so far. For an LP it is a liability (cash it must keep ready). For KKR it is future fee-paying capital. For a KCS pacing conversation, it is the number that decides how much more a client can commit this year INFERRED.

A real fund, read like an LP statement

KKR's 10-Q fund table gives the live state of European Fund VI at 30 June 2026: commitment $7,513m, uncalled $1,795m, invested $5,719m, remaining fair value $7,269m [S175] CONFIRMED. Read it the way an LP would. Called so far is about $7,513m minus $1,795m, roughly $5,718m, or 76% of commitments INFERRED. Remaining fair value of $7,269m against $5,719m invested is about 1.27x on paper, before any distributions already made INFERRED (the table does not separate realised proceeds).

Step 3: the management fee and its base

The fee base matters as much as the rate. KKR's drawdown private equity funds charge 1.0% to 2.0% of committed capital during the investment period, then 0.75% to 1.50% of invested capital afterwards [S2] CONFIRMED. Worked example, illustrative numbers within those ranges:

PhaseFormulaLP with $100m commitment
Investment period (6 years)rate × commitment = 1.5% × $100m$1.5m a year, $9.0m over 6 years, even in year 1 when only $20m is called
Year 1 effective ratefee ÷ cash called = $1.5m ÷ $20m7.5% of the cash actually at work
After investment periodrate × invested capital = 1.0% × $60m still invested$0.6m a year, falling as assets are sold

This is why large LPs negotiate the base as hard as the rate, and why fees on uncalled money show up in every institutional fee discussion INFERRED. It also explains a KKR-level number: committed capital that is not yet paying fees is "shadow" revenue that switches on when the fund starts its investment period [S1, S2] INFERRED.

Fee offsets. Portfolio companies can pay transaction or monitoring fees. Under KKR's post-2014 fund agreements, 100% of such fees allocable to the fund are shared with fund investors, in practice by reducing the management fee [S425] CONFIRMED.

Recycling. Many LPAs let the GP re-use ("recycle") early proceeds, for example from a deal sold within the investment period, instead of distributing them, which lets more than 100% of commitments be invested over the fund's life INFERRED (general practice; KKR's specific recycling terms are not in the sources). KKR's portfolio construction team makes the client-side version of the point: investors should use "recycled distributions" in pacing, noting that "annual distributions have historically averaged approximately 10-30% of NAV" [S436] CONFIRMED.

Step 4: the lifetime cash profile (the J-curve)

Step 5: the GP commitment

KKR also puts its own money in, so it shares the outcome. At 31 December 2025, KKR and its employees had about $30bn invested in or committed to KKR funds and companies: about $15bn funded from the balance sheet, $10bn more committed from it, $4bn of personal investments and $1bn more personally committed [S12] CONFIRMED. For GII IV, about $1bn came from KKR's balance sheet, affiliates and employees [S431] REPORTED; for DCIF, KKR committed at least $500m [S432] CONFIRMED. KKR says the relative size of GP commitments has declined as funds grew [S2] CONFIRMED. LPs test this in ILPA's due diligence questionnaire, whose section 10 is "Alignment of Interests" [S444] CONFIRMED.

2b. The distribution waterfall, worked in full@@l2-waterfall@@

When the fund sells a company, cash flows down a "waterfall": a fixed order of who is paid first. KKR's carried interest is typically 10% to 20% of fund profits, "usually after a 6% to 8% preferred return (hurdle) with a catch-up to the general partner" [S2] CONFIRMED. The four tiers of a European (whole-of-fund) waterfall are:

  1. Return of capital. 100% to LPs until they have back everything they paid in.
  2. Preferred return (hurdle). 100% to LPs until they have earned, say, 8% a year compounding on that capital.
  3. GP catch-up. 100% (in a "full" catch-up) to the GP until the GP has 20% of all profit paid so far.
  4. Split. Everything after that: 80% to LPs, 20% to the GP.

The worked example (illustrative numbers)

A $1,000m fund calls all its money on day one and receives all its proceeds in year 5. Terms: 8% compounding hurdle, 20% carry, 100% catch-up. These are within KKR's stated ranges [S2] but are not any specific fund's terms.

TierFormula in words and symbolsProceeds $1,800m
1 Return of capitalmin(proceeds, capital) = min(1,800, 1,000)LP 1,000.0; 800.0 left
2 Preferred returncapital × (1.085 minus 1) = 1,000 × 0.4693LP 469.3; 330.7 left
3 Catch-upGP takes C so that C = 20% × (469.3 + C), so C = 469.3 × 0.2 ÷ 0.8GP 117.3; 213.4 left
4 Split80% / 20% of what is leftLP 170.7; GP 42.7
TotalsCheck: profit 800 × 20% = 160LP 1,640.0 (1.64x); GP 160.0

Read the chart from top to bottom. At $1,300m the hurdle is not met, so KKR earns nothing. At $1,500m KKR is inside the catch-up and has only 6.1% of profit. From the point where catch-up ends (about $1,587m) KKR has exactly 20% of profit, which is the point of a full catch-up: it makes the hurdle a timing protection for LPs, not a permanent discount on carry INFERRED.

The American (deal-by-deal) contrast and the clawback

An American waterfall runs the tiers deal by deal, so the GP can receive carry on an early winner before the fund as a whole has made money. ILPA has long favoured whole-of-fund waterfalls plus a clawback, the GP's duty to hand back carry it turns out not to have earned; ILPA Principles 2.0 (2011) added a Clawback appendix [S317] CONFIRMED. Worked example, illustrative numbers: the same $1,000m fund invests $500m in deal A and $500m in deal B on day one.

This happened at KKR. In Q4 2025 KKR repaid $344m of carried interest on Asian Fund II (a 2013 vintage, $5.8bn fund); net of amounts recouped from current and former employees, the clawback cut net realised performance income by $207m [S2, S4] CONFIRMED. CalPERS reports a 1.0% net IRR and 1.0x multiple on its $350m commitment to that fund [S117] CONFIRMED. A clawback is not theory; it is a line in KKR's 2025 results INFERRED.

What you will learn

To trace a drawdown fund's cash from commitment to clawback: fee base by phase, the four waterfall tiers with the catch-up formula C = H × carry ÷ (1 minus carry), and why deal-by-deal timing changes value even when final splits match.

Where you meet it at KKR

Every flagship raise: GII V ($19.2bn, with investors from "pension plans, sovereign wealth funds, insurance companies, asset managers, private wealth platforms, and family offices") [S430] CONFIRMED. Clients re-up on the evidence of waterfalls like Asian Fund II's [S2, S117].

Only at KKR

Any GP can teach the textbook waterfall. KKR's filings let you check it against live numbers: the European Fund VI fund table [S175], a real $344m clawback [S2], and LP-reported returns from CalPERS [S117]. Learning from a firm that has just paid a clawback teaches why LPs insist on whole-of-fund terms INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to be able to walk a pension fund through a drawdown fund's life, from the capital call to the catch-up to KKR's own Asian Fund II clawback, in numbers rather than adjectives."

3. Evergreen mechanics: how K-PEC actually works@@l2-evergreen@@

A drawdown fund needs a $10m-plus cheque, a ten-year lock and an operations team to answer capital calls. Individuals cannot do that. An evergreen fund solves it: no end date, money goes in once, fully invested, and a limited amount can come out each quarter INFERRED. K-PEC, KKR Private Equity Conglomerate LLC, is the flagship. It is a Delaware LLC formed on 6 December 2022 that began operations on 1 August 2023 [S420] CONFIRMED.

The legal puzzle: why K-PEC is not an "investment company"

The US Investment Company Act of 1940 regulates funds that hold securities. If K-PEC were caught by it, the Act would restrict its leverage, its dealings with KKR affiliates and its fee structure [S420] CONFIRMED. The Act has two tests, and K-PEC's 10-K explains how it passes each [S420] CONFIRMED:

  • Test 1, "primarily engaged". A company is an investment company if it is "engaged primarily ... in the business of investing, reinvesting or trading in securities". K-PEC says that "through its Joint Ventures, the Company is primarily engaged in the businesses of its Joint Ventures, namely, the business of owning and controlling portfolio companies."
  • Test 2, the "40% test". A company is an investment company if "investment securities" exceed 40% of its total assets (excluding government securities and cash). K-PEC's main assets are wholly owned Operating Subsidiaries (for example K-PEC Holdings LLC). Those subsidiaries hold general partner interests in each deal-level Joint Venture, and "such general partner interests are not 'investment securities'." The only investment securities sit in the liquidity sleeve, which K-PEC keeps below 40%.

In plain English: a limited partner owns a passive stake (a security); a general partner runs the partnership (an operating business). By making K-PEC a co-general partner of each deal, alongside the KKR flagship fund, KKR turned a fund into an operating company, an old-style conglomerate [S420] INFERRED. The structure has teeth: if the Management Agreement with KKR ends, K-PEC must "forfeit our controlling interest in any Joint Venture, which would likely require us to register as an investment company" [S420] CONFIRMED.

Allocation: who gets the deal first

K-PEC co-invests with KKR's flagship private equity funds, but it is at the back of the queue. Its 10-K says: "the Company does not benefit from any mandatory minimum purchase rights or minimum purchase thresholds. As such the Company will not benefit from any priority acquisition allocation." Flagship funds have a mandatory minimum that is filled first. K-PEC is then offered a "first tier" alongside nearly all KKR vehicles "up to a capped amount", and may then be offered part of any "second tier" [S420] CONFIRMED. The institutional flagship LP gets first call; the wealth vehicle fills in behind INFERRED.

K-PEC also bought stakes in two companies in 2025 ($165.5m) "from affiliated KKR-sponsored investment vehicles alongside the relevant flagship private equity vehicle as part of a sale and purchase between successive fund vintages" [S420] CONFIRMED. That is the kind of related-party transfer ILPA's due diligence questionnaire probes in its section on GP-led secondaries [S444] INFERRED.

NAV: how the price is struck every month

NAV (net asset value) is the value of everything the fund owns minus what it owes, divided by shares. For K-PEC, the Manager "determines the NAV of our Shares monthly" and an Administrator computes the per-share figure [S420] CONFIRMED. All portfolio companies are Level III, meaning there is no market price, so value comes from models and comparables; K-PEC discloses that a 10% fall in their fair value would cut net assets by $898.6m [S420] CONFIRMED. The price investors deal at is the Transactional NAV, which differs from accounting (GAAP) NAV mainly because GAAP books the lifetime cost of distribution fees up front: $9.932bn Transactional versus $9.495bn GAAP at 31 December 2025 [S420] CONFIRMED, a gap of $437m INFERRED.

Subscribing: you buy dollars, not shares

K-PEC runs "a continuous private offering of our Shares on a monthly basis" [S420] CONFIRMED. An investor subscribes a dollar amount and only learns the share count afterwards: a subscription accepted on 1 September is priced at the 31 August Transactional NAV [S420] CONFIRMED. Worked example using the Class I NAV per share at 31 December 2025, $33.80 [S420] CONFIRMED:

StepClass I (no load)Class S (illustrative full load)
Cheque$100,000$100,000
Upfront sales load and dealer-manager feenoneup to 3.00% + 0.50%, capped at 3.50% [S420]: $3,500
Invested at $33.802,958.6 shares$96,500 ÷ 33.80 = 2,855.0 shares
Ongoing distribution and servicing feenone0.85% of NAV a year (0.60% + 0.25%), paid to KKR Capital Markets as dealer-manager [S420]

How the load is applied in practice depends on the selling agreement; the table shows the maximum. The share classes map to sales channels: Class S through brokers who take a commission, Class D through fee-based advisers, Class I through institutional or advisory accounts INFERRED (the 10-K does not define the channel per class).

3b. The evergreen fee stack and the repurchase plan, worked@@l2-evfees@@

The two fees

Management fee: 1.25% a year of month-end NAV for Classes S, D, U and I, paid monthly. The early "R" classes pay 1.00% for 60 months from 1 August 2023, then 1.25% [S420] CONFIRMED. Performance Participation Allocation (the evergreen version of carry): "15.0% of the Total Return attributable to Investor Shares subject to a 5.0% annual Hurdle Amount and a High Water Mark, with a 100% Catch-Up", measured and paid annually and accrued monthly [S420] CONFIRMED.

Key terms

Hurdle: the return investors keep before KKR shares in any gain (here 5% a year). Catch-up: once over the hurdle, KKR takes 100% of the next slice until it has 15% of the whole return. High-water mark: KKR earns nothing on gains that only recover earlier losses (the 10-K's "Loss Carryforward Amount") [S420] CONFIRMED (plain-English gloss INFERRED).

One year, worked (illustrative numbers)

An investor holds $1,000,000 of Class I at the start of the year. The portfolio gains $200,000 before fees. Average NAV during the year is about $1,100,000.

StepFormulaAmount
Management fee1.25% × average NAV ≈ 1.25% × 1,100,000$13,750
Total Return before performance allocation200,000 minus 13,750$186,250 (18.6%)
Hurdle (investor keeps)5% × 1,000,000$50,000
Catch-up (100% to KKR)C = 15% × (50,000 + C), so C = 7,500 ÷ 0.85$8,824
Split of the rest, 85 / 15186,250 minus 58,824 = 127,426; KKR 15%KKR $19,114; investor $108,312
KKR's performance allocationCheck: 15% × 186,250$27,938
Investor keeps186,250 minus 27,938$158,312 (15.8%)

For comparison, K-PEC's actual 2025 total return was 16.81% for Class I and 14.80% for Class D; inception-to-date annualised return for Class I was 12.94% since September 2023 [S420] CONFIRMED. Now the two edge cases. If the Total Return is 4%, it is below the hurdle and KKR takes nothing. If it is 5.5% ($55,000), the investor keeps the first $50,000 and KKR takes all $5,000 above it, because that slice is inside the catch-up zone INFERRED. In a year after a loss, say minus $100,000, the next $100,000 of gain is a recovery under the high-water mark and attracts no performance allocation [S420] (numbers illustrative).

The repurchase plan: the exit door and its size

K-PEC lets investors sell shares back quarterly, but the door is narrow [S420] CONFIRMED:

  • The cap (gate): total repurchases of "no more than 5.0% of our aggregate NAV ... per calendar quarter". The Board can repurchase less, or nothing.
  • Early Repurchase Fee: 5.0% of NAV on shares held under 24 months, kept by the fund for the remaining holders; it may be waived on death, disability or divorce.
  • Overflow: if requests exceed 5%, the Board may run a tender for "Excess Shares" "at or about a 10% discount to the most recent NAV". This is unusual: a paid exit valve above the gate INFERRED.
  • Minimum balance after a partial repurchase: $1,000.

In practice K-PEC's door has been barely used so far: 2025 repurchases were about $26.4m net of early repurchase fees against $9.9bn of NAV [S420] CONFIRMED, roughly 0.3% for the year INFERRED. The fund also has a third-party revolving credit facility upsized to $1.0bn in March 2026, with recourse borrowing capped at 30% of total assets [S420] CONFIRMED; a credit line is a further buffer between a redemption request and a forced sale INFERRED.

3c. The rest of the K-Series: three more legal answers@@l2-kseries@@

K-INFRA uses the same conglomerate design for infrastructure. It owns "Infrastructure Assets" through Joint Ventures with Global Infrastructure Investors II to V, Asia Pacific Infrastructure Investors I to III, DCIF and the Global Climate Fund. Target mix about 85% infrastructure (no more than 15% non-OECD) and 15% liquidity. Fees: 1.25% of NAV plus 12.5% of Total Return over a 5% hurdle, with a high-water mark and 100% catch-up. Repurchases quarterly, capped at 5% of NAV, 5% fee within 24 months. Distributions are quarterly, and the 10-K warns they "may and are expected to exceed our earnings and cash flow from operating activities" [S421] CONFIRMED. K-INFRA can also buy "Warehoused Assets" that KKR or a bank bought first, "at cost, or cost plus an interest rate or carrying cost" [S421] CONFIRMED: the same buy-then-syndicate engine KCM runs for institutional co-investors INFERRED.

K-FIT takes the opposite legal route. It is inside the 1940 Act framework, as "an externally managed, non-diversified, closed-end management investment company that has elected to be regulated as a business development company", and is a RIC for tax, paying no corporate tax if it distributes its income [S422] CONFIRMED. Its adviser, FS/KKR Advisor, is jointly operated by KKR Credit and an affiliate of Future Standard (formerly FS Investments) [S422] CONFIRMED. Rules: at least 60% of assets in first lien senior secured loans to US middle-market companies; up to 30% in asset-based finance and subordinated loans; quarterly tender for "up to 5% of our Common Shares outstanding"; a 2% Early Repurchase Deduction within 12 months [S422] CONFIRMED. Its fee is income-based, worked below.

Why the catch-up ends at 1.43%: the adviser has caught up when its fee equals 12.5% of all income, so (I minus 1.25) = 0.125 × I, which gives I = 1.25 ÷ 0.875 = 1.4286% INFERRED. K-FIT's leverage limit is the BDC rule of 150% asset coverage, meaning assets must be at least 1.5 times debt, which allows debt of up to roughly 2x equity [S422] CONFIRMED ("2x" is INFERRED). At 31 December 2025 it had total assets of $2.75bn and shareholders' equity of $1.54bn [S422] CONFIRMED, so liabilities of about $1.21bn, or about 0.8x equity, well inside the limit INFERRED.

KREST is a third answer: a registered closed-end fund under the 1940 Act (the N-2 cover ticks "Registered Closed-End Fund", not "Interval Fund"). It invests at least 80% of net assets in real estate. Minimum initial investment is $10,000 for Classes S, D and U and $1,000,000 for Class I, with $500 subsequent; it is the only K-Series vehicle with a published minimum in the sources. Fees: 1.25% of average daily net assets plus 12.5% of quarterly "Portfolio Operating Income", with no incentive fee on capital gains. It "intends, but is not obligated, to conduct quarterly tender offers for up to 5.0% of the aggregate NAV", and admits repurchases "have in the past been and may in the future be oversubscribed" [S424] CONFIRMED.

What you will learn

To explain the 40% test in one breath, to price a monthly subscription, to compute a year's performance allocation with hurdle, catch-up and high-water mark, and to work a pro-rated quarter with early repurchase fees and an overflow tender.

Where you meet it at KKR

London product management for K-PRIME and K-PEC [S133] CONFIRMED; the UK launch of KKR's evergreen private equity strategy with Quilter Cheviot from January 2026 [S177] CONFIRMED; and KKR's earnings, where K-Series private equity performance fees (about $160m in Q2 2026) now sit in Fee Related Earnings [S332] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

Evergreen private equity is not unique to KKR: Blackstone's BXPE raised $2.4bn in Q2 2026 alone [S213] CONFIRMED. What is specific is the co-general-partner Joint Venture route around the 40% test, explained in K-PEC's own 10-K [S420], and a London team that manages both the US LLC and its Luxembourg twin [S133]. Learning both at once shows how one strategy is rebuilt for two legal systems INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to understand how K-PEC turns a buyout fund into an operating company through co-GP joint ventures, and how its 5% quarterly door, hurdle and catch-up actually feel to the adviser whose client owns it."

4. Interval funds: the public-private blend@@l2-interval@@

An interval fund is a US registered fund that continuously sells shares and, under SEC Rule 23c-3, adopts a "fundamental policy" (changeable only by a shareholder vote) to make quarterly repurchase offers for a set share of its stock. Capital Group KKR Core Plus+ commits to offers of "between 5% and 25% of its outstanding shares at net asset value" and "currently expects to offer to repurchase 10%"; oversubscribed offers are pro-rated [S426] CONFIRMED. The key contrast: an interval fund must make the offer every quarter; a K-Series board may repurchase less or nothing [S420, S426] INFERRED.

Why blend public and private?

A fund that promises 10% liquidity a quarter cannot hold only assets that take months to sell. So the blend is the liquidity engine. Core Plus+ allocates "approximately 60% of its net assets to public debt assets (to be managed by the investment adviser) and approximately 40% to private credit assets (to be managed by the sub-adviser)"; the adviser is Capital Research and Management Company and the sub-adviser KKR Credit Advisors (US) LLC, paid "solely by the investment adviser out of the investment adviser's fees" [S426] CONFIRMED. The minimum is "normally $1,000 per account", the management fee 0.61%, and total expenses after a 0.30% waiver range from 0.84% (F-3, R-6) to 1.54% (A-2) [S426] CONFIRMED.

A simple stress test shows why the private share stops at 40% (illustrative numbers) INFERRED:

Vehicle (stylised)Liquid sleeveMaximum quarterly exitQuarters of maximum exits the liquid sleeve covers with no new money
Core Plus+ style interval fund, $1bn60% = $600m10% = $100m (expected offer)6
U.S. Equity+ style interval fund, $1bn60% = $600m5% = $50m12
K-PEC style evergreen, $1bnup to 20% = $200m5% = $50m (board may pay less)4

The arithmetic ignores rebalancing, inflows and the credit line, but it shows the trade-off clearly: firmer liquidity costs private exposure INFERRED. Capital Group's own warehouse makes the launch work: a financing provider pre-bought private credit; once the fund reaches $150m of net assets it must buy up to $2m of those assets, rising to $8m at $400m and up to 2% of net assets at $500m [S426] CONFIRMED. That is how a new fund starts with a seasoned private portfolio INFERRED.

U.S. Equity+: a fund that owns K-PEC

Capital Group KKR U.S. Equity+ is also an interval fund, with "approximately 60% of its net assets in publicly traded equity securities and approximately 40% of its net assets in private equity securities". The 40% is split: "Normally, the fund expects to invest approximately 30% of its net assets in K-PEC and approximately 10% of its net assets in co-investments" alongside K-PEC or other KKR private equity vehicles. It buys K-PEC units monthly like any investor, and its K-PEC stake can only be redeemed through K-PEC's 5%-per-quarter plan. It makes quarterly offers "for 5% of its outstanding shares under ordinary circumstances", and charges a fund-level management fee of 0.350% [S427] CONFIRMED. A third-party analysis says it opened on 2 March 2026 [S364] REPORTED (low-authority source).

What you will learn

How Rule 23c-3 turns liquidity into a legal promise, how to size the liquid sleeve that backs it, and how to look through a fund-of-KKR product to the underlying deals, fees and exit doors.

Where you meet it at KKR

The Capital Group partnership: Nuttall said it extends KKR's "reach and our distribution relationships", adding that Capital Group has "relationships with 220,000 of the 300,000 advisers in the United States" [S362] REPORTED. KKR's 10-K notes the Capital Group equity product "invests in a K-Series private equity vehicle" [S70] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

Other alternatives firms partner with traditional managers, so the concept is not unique; the Capital Group pairing is KKR's alone [S210, S201] CONFIRMED (uniqueness of the pairing INFERRED). Seeing K-PEC used as an ingredient inside someone else's interval fund is a rare view of the same product from both ends INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn why Capital Group KKR U.S. Equity+ can only put 40% into private equity, and what it means that a third of that 40% is redeemable only through K-PEC's own quarterly door."

5. Liquidity mismatch and the toolkit@@l2-liquidity@@

The core problem of every semi-liquid fund in one sentence: the assets take years to sell, but the investors can ask for money every quarter. If everyone asks at once, the fund must either sell assets cheaply (hurting those who stay), pay some investors and not others, or say no. Each tool below is a rule decided in advance about which of those happens INFERRED.

The tools one by one

  • Gate. A cap on total exits per period. Without it, the first investors to leave get cash from the liquid sleeve and those who stay end up owning only illiquid assets INFERRED.
  • Pro-rata. If requests exceed the gate, everyone gets the same fraction. In the crowded quarter worked above, 62.5%.
  • Redemption fee. Charged to leavers and kept in the fund. It compensates stayers for the cost of raising cash and discourages short-term money [S420] INFERRED.
  • Liquidity sleeve. Cash and tradable credit held to meet exits; it drags returns in good times, which is why it is capped at about 15 to 20% in the K-Series [S420, S421] CONFIRMED.
  • NAV lag. K-PEC strikes NAV monthly from Level III models and deals at the prior month-end price [S420]. If markets fall sharply mid-month, a leaver may exit at a stale, higher price. Funds that trade on NAV are exactly where operational due diligence focuses: Cambridge Associates stresses open-end and evergreen funds "where transactions are based on the fund's NAV" and that "Fee calculations rely on accurate valuations" [S458] CONFIRMED.

The live example: K-FIT's queue in Q1 2026

In Q1 2026 investors in K-FIT "tried to redeem 6.3% of shares in the prior quarter, causing the fund to prorate requests at about 80%"; in Q2 requests fell to 1.65% and were paid in full [S338] REPORTED. The arithmetic checks: 5% cap ÷ 6.3% requested = 79% INFERRED. It was part of an industry wave: private credit redemption requests topped $20bn in Q1 2026 [S337] REPORTED. Apollo Debt Solutions received requests of 11.2% and honoured 5%, Blue Owl enforced a 5% cap, and Blackstone's BCRED paid 100% of record requests of 7.9% of NAV [S278] REPORTED. Partners Group said requests at its Delaware private equity evergreen fund would be "slightly above the 5%" limit [S339] REPORTED.

Two lessons for a client team. First, a gate working as designed is not a default: it is the contract doing what it said. Second, the reputational cost lands on whoever set expectations at the point of sale INFERRED. KCS juniors are the people who draft the investor letters and adviser FAQs when a queue forms INFERRED.

The European and UK rulebooks

AIFMD II (EU). Open-ended alternative investment funds must select "at least two appropriate LMTs" (liquidity management tools) from points 2 to 8 of Annex V: redemption gates, extension of notice periods, redemption fees, swing pricing, dual pricing, anti-dilution levies and redemption in kind. Suspension and side pockets sit outside the count as exceptional tools. "This cannot be done by selecting only dual and swing pricing", and redemptions in kind are for professional investors only [S449] CONFIRMED [S450] REPORTED. The Level 2 rules (Commission Delegated Regulation (EU) 2026/465) apply from 16 April 2026; funds constituted before that date are "deemed to comply with the RTS until 16 April 2027" [S449] CONFIRMED. ESMA recommends, but does not require, "at least one quantitative LMT and one Anti-Dilution Tool" [S448] CONFIRMED. Worked example (illustrative): gate plus notice period is a valid pair; swing pricing plus dual pricing is not.

ELTIF 2.0 (EU). The redemption rules (Delegated Regulation (EU) 2024/2759, in force 26 October 2024) set no fixed minimum holding period; redemptions more often than quarterly must be justified; a notice period under 3 months must be explained to the regulator; and matching exiting with incoming investors is allowed under a policy [S447] CONFIRMED. The maximum redemption is sized by one of two methods [S447] CONFIRMED:

MethodRule from the RTS exampleWorked: €500m fund with €100m of liquid assets (illustrative)
Annex I: frequency + notice periodQuarterly with 3 months' notice: up to 33.3% of liquid assets0.333 × €100m = €33.3m a quarter, 6.7% of NAV
Annex II: frequency + minimum liquid assetsQuarterly with at least 20% liquid assets: up to 50%0.50 × €100m = €50m a quarter, 10% of NAV

The liquid-asset base also includes cash flows forecast prudently over 12 months [S447] CONFIRMED. The ELTIF market reached about €34bn at end-2025 with 189 new ELTIFs since 2024 [S302] REPORTED.

Source uncertain

UK LTAF. Secondary sources describe the Long-Term Asset Fund as open-ended with dealing no more often than monthly and a notice period of at least 90 days [S451] REPORTED; the FCA rulebook (COLL 15) itself was not read. The LTAF has been open to retail investors as a Restricted Mass Market Investment since 3 July 2023 [S305] CONFIRMED. An FCA speech in May 2026 put LTAF assets at £7.5bn, up from £5bn in under a year [S452] REPORTED. No KKR ELTIF or LTAF was found in the sources; EQT launched an evergreen infrastructure ELTIF in April 2026 [S464] REPORTED.

What you will learn

To diagnose a liquidity mismatch, choose and size the tools (gate, pro-rata, fee, sleeve, notice, matching), and map them to the rulebook in each market: SEC Rule 23c-3, AIFMD II Annex V, the ELTIF RTS and the LTAF.

Where you meet it at KKR

K-FIT's Q1 2026 pro-rating [S338]; K-PRIME in Luxembourg, which the sources describe as an open-ended Part II UCI [S429] REPORTED, so its toolkit sits under European rules INFERRED; and the FCA's Consumer Duty, under which a UK distributor of a KKR fund needs target market and fair value information from KKR [S452] REPORTED (link to KKR INFERRED).

Only at KKR

KKR runs the full range of doors on one shelf: mandatory quarterly offers (Capital Group interval funds), discretionary 5% plans with an overflow tender (K-PEC), a BDC tender (K-FIT), and multi-year institutional lock-ups (DCIF) [S420, S422, S426, S432]. A London seat also adds the EU and UK rulebooks. Few places let a junior compare all of these from the same desk INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"After K-FIT paid about 80% of requests in Q1 2026 and 100% in Q2, I want to learn how a client team explains a gate before the queue forms, not after."

6. The same strategy, three wrappers: KKR infrastructure@@l2-wrappers@@

Put everything together on one asset class. KKR manages about $120bn of infrastructure equity, "up from just $13 billion in 2019", with about 160 dedicated infrastructure executives [S430] CONFIRMED. The same team sells that exposure through three very different wrappers. KKR itself counts them together: GII V contributes to "approximately $45 billion raised across KKR's latest infrastructure vehicle vintages globally", a figure that includes infrastructure perpetual vehicles such as K-INFRA [S430] CONFIRMED.

The three wrappers, row by row

The full-page figure below sets them side by side; here is how to read it.

Who each serves. GII V is for institutions that can commit for a decade and manage capital calls; its investor list includes "private wealth platforms" too, which typically enter through feeders [S430] CONFIRMED (feeder route INFERRED). DCIF is for institutions that want steady, yield-like infrastructure with "low volatility and strong downside protection" and are willing to accept a three-year hard lock [S432] CONFIRMED. K-INFRA is for individuals who need a monthly price and some quarterly liquidity [S421] CONFIRMED.

Why the fees differ. The institutional open-ended core fund charges 0.6% to 0.8% with carry only on cash yield; the wealth vehicle charges 1.25% plus 12.5% over 5% [S421, S432] CONFIRMED. The wealth price pays for things institutions do themselves: monthly valuation and administration, the liquidity sleeve, adviser education, and distribution through platforms INFERRED. KKR's own fee table shows the same split across products: open-ended vehicles at 0.50% to 1.25% versus drawdown infrastructure at 0.75% to 1.50% [S2] CONFIRMED.

Why the performance fee is lower than K-PEC's. K-INFRA's 12.5% versus K-PEC's 15% matches the lower expected return of infrastructure [S421] CONFIRMED (interpretation INFERRED). K-INFRA's 2025 Class I return was 10.13% against K-PEC's 16.81% [S420, S421] CONFIRMED.

Why it matters to a client conversation. A family office asking "should I buy KKR infrastructure?" is really asking three questions: can you meet capital calls for six years, can you accept a multi-year lock, or do you need a quarterly door? The answer picks the wrapper, and the wrapper sets the fee INFERRED.

What you will learn

To present one strategy in three wrappers side by side, explain every row of the trade-off (fee base, carry design, lock-up, door, cash profile), and match the wrapper to a client's liquidity and operational capacity.

Where you meet it at KKR

The London infrastructure product team, which includes a specialist for "infra for individuals" [S150] CONFIRMED; GII V's investor base [S430]; and K-INFRA's Joint Ventures with GII and DCIF [S421]. Daniel Valladares moved from the infrastructure product team to Head of EMEA Consultant Relations [S132] CONFIRMED, the people who read memos like Cliffwater's [S432].

Only at KKR

At KKR the three wrappers are literally linked: K-INFRA's 10-K names GII II to V and DCIF as its Joint Venture partners [S421] CONFIRMED, and GII V's $45bn platform figure counts the perpetual vehicles alongside the drawdown funds [S430] CONFIRMED. Rivals also sell infrastructure in several wrappers (EQT's evergreen ELTIF, for one [S464] REPORTED); what you learn at KKR is how one asset is shared across all three by contract INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn to put GII V, DCIF and K-INFRA side by side and explain to a client why the same infrastructure costs 0.8% in one wrapper and 1.25% plus 12.5% in another, and when each price is fair."

Module 1 in one paragraph

A private markets product is a contract about four things: who may buy, when cash moves, how fees are charged and how you get out. Drawdown funds answer with commitments, capital calls, a fee on committed capital and a whole-of-fund waterfall with a clawback. Evergreen funds answer with a monthly NAV, a fee on NAV, a hurdle with catch-up and high-water mark, and a narrow quarterly door. Interval funds add a legal promise of liquidity and pay for it with a large public sleeve. When too many people head for the door, gates, pro-rating, fees and notice periods decide who bears the cost. KKR runs every one of these pipes, and often runs them into the same deals INFERRED.

@@l3@@
Part 3

Module 2: Thinking like the client: portfolio construction and performance

Before KKR can sell a client anything, someone has to understand the client's whole portfolio better than the client's own spreadsheet does. This module teaches that spreadsheet: liabilities, allocation, pacing, performance maths and insurance capital, each with numbers you can redo by hand.

Part 1 profiled six client types on five constraints. This module goes one level down and puts formulas on them. Every section follows the same pattern: the mechanism from zero, a worked example with numbers, a diagram, then where the same mechanism shows up in real KKR material. Numbers marked "illustrative" are invented to teach the arithmetic; every other number carries its source.

Five words you need before you start

Liability: money a client must pay out in future (pensions, insurance claims, a family's spending). Asset allocation: the split of a portfolio between types of investment. Commitment: a promise to give a private fund money when it asks. NAV (net asset value): what a holding is worth today on the manager's valuation. Benchmark: the yardstick a result is compared against.

Liabilities first: what "enough" means to each client@@l3-liabilities@@

An investment professional's first question is never "what will make the most money?" It is "what does this money have to do, and when?" The answer is set by the client's liabilities. Return and liquidity targets are derived from them, not chosen freely.

Two jobs a portfolio can do

Liability matching means owning assets whose cash flows or values move with what you owe. If a pension must pay £50m in 2035, a government bond that pays £50m in 2035 removes that risk: whatever interest rates do, the asset and the bill move together. Return seeking means owning assets expected to grow faster than the liabilities, such as shares or private equity, accepting that their value swings. Every institutional portfolio is a blend of the two. The blend is the single most important decision a client makes, and it decides how much room there is for private markets INFERRED.

Key terms

Funding ratio: assets divided by the present value of liabilities. 100% means the client can pay every promise today. Present value: what a future payment is worth today once you discount it at an interest rate. LDI (liability-driven investment): a strategy, mostly with bonds and interest rate hedges, that makes assets move with liabilities. Liquidity budget: how much cash the client must be able to raise, how quickly, in a bad month.

Worked example 1: a DB pension's "enough return" (illustrative numbers)

A UK company pension has £1.0bn of assets. Its promises to members are worth £1.1bn in present value. Assume its liabilities grow 5% a year (interest on the promises plus new accrual, net of payments; illustrative).

  1. Funding ratio = assets ÷ liabilities = 1.0 ÷ 1.1 = 90.9%. The deficit is £100m.
  2. Goal: be fully funded in 10 years with no extra cash from the employer. Assets must reach the liabilities' future value: 1.0 × (1 + r)10 = 1.1 × 1.0510.
  3. Divide both sides by 1.0 and take the tenth root: 1 + r = 1.05 × 1.11/10 = 1.05 × 1.00958 = 1.0601.
  4. "Enough return" = 6.0% a year. Of that, 5.0 points just keep pace with the liabilities and about 1.0 point repairs the deficit.

The lesson: this scheme does not need 12%. It needs 6.0% with as little chance as possible of a big miss. If it hedges most of its liabilities with bonds that earn roughly the 5% liability growth, the remaining "growth" assets have to earn the extra. Private markets compete for that small growth slice, which is why a mature DB scheme is a shrinking buyer of private equity and a natural buyer of contracted, income-producing assets such as core infrastructure or investment-grade private credit INFERRED. KKR's own institutional page offers corporate pensions "consultative advice to corporations who are considering the benefits and challenges associated with de-risking their defined benefit plans" [S81] CONFIRMED.
