Worked example 2: a liquidity budget (illustrative numbers)

The same £1.0bn pension pays £60m of pensions a year (£5m a month). Its hedges can demand cash collateral if gilt yields jump; assume a stress call of £80m. It also has £70m of unfunded private commitments that could be called within a year. Stress cash need = 60 + 80 + 70 = £210m. Assets it can sell within a month at a fair price: cash £20m plus listed shares £300m plus a share of the gilts not tied up as collateral, say £100m: £420m. Liquidity coverage = 420 ÷ 210 = 2.0x. If the board's rule is "at least 1.5x", the scheme can afford more illiquid assets only until coverage falls to 1.5x: it could move up to £105m more into private assets, assuming that money comes from the listed shares (each £1 moved cuts liquid assets by £1: 420 − 1.5 × 210 = 105). This is the sum a KCS junior should be able to do before suggesting a commitment size INFERRED.

What you will learn

To derive a client's required return and liquidity budget from its liabilities: funding ratio, required return to close a deficit, liquidity coverage under stress. Then to say how much room that leaves for private markets, before any product is mentioned.

Where you meet it at KKR

KKR's institutional client segments are corporate pensions, public pensions, sovereign wealth funds, insurers, endowments and foundations [S81] CONFIRMED. Pensions and other institutions are 14% of KKR's AUM, sovereigns 12%, third-party insurers 11% and Global Atlantic's balance sheet 22% (31 Dec 2025) [S71] CONFIRMED. Each segment brings a different definition of "enough".

Only at KKR

KKR runs an insurer (Global Atlantic) on its own balance sheet, so liability-first thinking is not theory inside the firm: Global Atlantic's $220bn of AUM must match real annuity promises (30 Jun 2026) [S1] CONFIRMED. A KCS team that sells to pensions and insurers sits next to colleagues who manage liabilities every day. Most fund managers have no liabilities of their own to learn from INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn to start from a client's liabilities, to work out the return and liquidity they actually need, and only then ask which KKR strategy fits."

Strategic asset allocation and KKR's Total Portfolio Approach@@l3-tpa@@

Strategic asset allocation (SAA) from zero

Most institutions run a strategic asset allocation: the board fixes a long-term target weight for each asset class ("bucket"), say 50% listed equity, 30% bonds, 8% private equity, 6% infrastructure, 6% real estate. Each bucket has its own team, its own benchmark and a tolerance band (for example 6% to 10% for private equity). When a bucket drifts outside its band, the client buys or sells to rebalance. SAA is simple to govern: the board can see at a glance whether the portfolio is "on policy" INFERRED.

The weakness is that buckets are defined by legal form, not by risk. A private credit loan, a listed high-yield bond and a slice of infrastructure debt can carry very similar risks yet sit in three buckets with three benchmarks. An opportunity that fits no bucket (a capital solutions deal, a reinsurance sidecar) may have no home at all INFERRED.

The Total Portfolio Approach (TPA): what KKR actually published

KKR's Multi-Asset Strategies team published "The Total Portfolio Approach: A Practical Framework" in February 2026, by Henry H. McVey, David McNellis and Christian Olinger [S435, S462] CONFIRMED. A follow-up, "The Dog Has Caught the Bus: The Total Portfolio Approach", appeared in July 2026, adding Amy Dai as an author [S436] CONFIRMED. McVey is Head of Global Macro & Asset Allocation and CIO of KKR's Balance Sheet; McNellis is Co-Head of Global Macro and Head of Portfolio Construction and Multi-Asset Strategies for Private Markets [S436] CONFIRMED.

  • Definition. TPA is "a whole-portfolio decision and governance framework that evaluates investment opportunities across Public and Private Markets based on their contributions to total portfolio objectives, rather than within static asset-class sleeves" [S435] CONFIRMED.
  • Governance. "The board sets a reference portfolio (for instance, 70% Public Equity/30% Fixed Income) and risk appetite ... and delegates allocation authority to the CIO within a total portfolio risk budget" [S435] CONFIRMED.
  • The contrast table. SAA uses fixed asset-class targets, many benchmarks and diversification "Through asset classes"; TPA uses dynamic weights, a "Single reference portfolio and risk budget" and diversification "Through risk factors" [S435] CONFIRMED.
  • The analogy KKR uses with clients: TPA is "zero-based budgeting for portfolio construction ... if every investment had to earn its place in the portfolio today, how would capital be allocated?" [S436] CONFIRMED.
  • The on-ramp: a "minimum viable TPA", which "can include centralizing portfolio-level functions, strengthening in-house portfolio construction and relative-value capabilities, refreshing views more frequently, and piloting TPA through a dedicated sleeve that has clear risk and liquidity guardrails" [S435] CONFIRMED.

Worked example 3: the TPA "funding rule" (illustrative numbers)

The core TPA move is to ask what an investment replaces. Suppose analysis shows a private credit fund behaves, in risk terms, like 30% listed equity plus 70% bonds (an illustrative factor mix, not a KKR estimate). Assume expected returns of 7.0% for listed equity and 4.5% for bonds.

  1. Fund it from its twin. To add £100m of private credit without changing the portfolio's risk, sell £30m of equity and £70m of bonds.
  2. Its hurdle is the twin's return: 0.30 × 7.0% + 0.70 × 4.5% = 2.10% + 3.15% = 5.25%.
  3. Value added: if the private credit is expected to earn 8.0% after fees, the gain is (8.0% − 5.25%) × £100m = £2.75m a year, for roughly the same risk, paid for with illiquidity.
  4. The SAA contrast: in a bucket model the same loan sits in an "alternatives" bucket, measured against its own peer benchmark, and nobody checks what it displaced.

KKR's paper runs the grown-up version: an optimiser matches the factor exposures of a "70% Equities/30% Bonds reference portfolio" while allowing "up to 30% of total portfolio capital to be allocated to Private Markets" and "up to 10% total portfolio leverage" [S435] CONFIRMED.

Why TPA matters for private markets in particular

KKR argues TPA gives private markets investors "improved liquidity management, steadier commitment pacing, better vintage diversification, and greater flexibility through drawdowns and denominator effects" [S435] CONFIRMED. The July paper lists three hard problems for private assets inside TPA: the opportunity set and relative value; risk measurement, because "Investors often commit capital before knowing the exposures they will ultimately own"; and dynamism, meaning illiquidity [S436] CONFIRMED. Its answers include strategy-level benchmarks, public comparables, pacing with recycled distributions, and "secondaries and liquidity facilities available during dislocations" [S436] CONFIRMED. It also gives the key pacing input: "Across private market strategies, annual distributions have historically averaged approximately 10-30% of NAV" [S436] CONFIRMED. You will use that number in the next section.

KKR's model portfolios: how much private, in KKR's own published numbers

For financial advisers, KKR's Global Macro & Asset Allocation team publishes three illustrative model portfolios on kkr.com/wealth, built from Burgiss, Bloomberg and NCREIF data, with "No specific KKR products referenced" [S437] CONFIRMED. The arithmetic of their weights gives 45% equity / 25% bonds / 30% private for the Growth model, and 30% / 40% / 30% for the Income and Capital Preservation models [S437] INFERRED.

Get this right

KKR publishes no "40/30/30" model. If anyone (or any summary you read) says KKR recommends 40% equity / 30% bonds / 30% private, correct it: the published models are 45/25/30 (Growth) and 30/40/30 (Income and Capital Preservation) [S437] CONFIRMED; totals derived INFERRED. The common thread is 30% in private markets for a balanced portfolio. KKR's case also rests on two claims on the same page: stock-bond correlations "have risen meaningfully, reducing the diversification benefits that once anchored the 60/40", and "the number of publicly listed U.S. companies has declined by nearly 50%" since 2000 [S437] CONFIRMED.

What you will learn

How institutions decide weights (SAA, bands, rebalancing), how TPA replaces buckets with a reference portfolio and a risk budget, and how to compute an investment's hurdle from what it displaces. Also how KKR translates the same logic into adviser-friendly model portfolios.

Where you meet it at KKR

The two TPA papers [S435, S436] and the TPA FAQ page [S463] are client-facing content: KKR says the TPA analogy "consistently resonated with clients" [S436] CONFIRMED. The model portfolios sit on kkr.com/wealth next to the "Alternatives Unlocked" courses [S437, S465] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

KKR's TPA authors also run the firm's own balance sheet (McVey is its CIO) [S436] CONFIRMED, and KKR says portfolio-level exposure observations "inform future deployment decisions, including where to lean in and where to syndicate through co-investment" [S436] CONFIRMED. So the framework KCS takes to clients is also how KKR allocates its own capital. Elsewhere, TPA papers usually come from consultants or asset owners who do not originate the private assets themselves INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to understand how a CIO decides what earns a place in a total portfolio, using KKR's own Total Portfolio Approach work, so I can explain why a private allocation belongs there, not just what it is."

Commitment pacing: why you cannot just "buy 10% private equity"@@l3-pacing@@

With listed shares, deciding on a 10% weight and owning 10% are the same day's work. With a private fund they are years apart. A client commits money; the fund calls it in instalments as deals are found (typically over 3 to 5 years for KKR's drawdown funds [S70] CONFIRMED); it distributes cash as assets are sold; and the client's actual exposure is the NAV in between. So the client controls commitments, but its target is set in NAV. Converting one into the other is called commitment pacing, and the standard tool is a 2002 model from Yale INFERRED.

The Takahashi-Alexander ("Yale") model

Dean Takahashi and Seth Alexander published "Illiquid Alternative Asset Fund Modeling" in the Journal of Portfolio Management in 2002 [S454] CONFIRMED. It is "a discrete-time deterministic model" (it steps year by year and has no randomness) with six inputs: "RC (rate of contribution), CC (capital commitment), L (life of the fund), B (bow factor describing changes on the rate of distribution over time), G (annual growth rate), and Y (yield percentage)" [S454] CONFIRMED. In words: each year the fund calls a share of what is still uncalled; the NAV grows; a rising share of the NAV is paid out, and the bow sets how back-loaded that payout is [S455] REPORTED.

The four equations, in symbols and in words

1. Calls: Ct = RCt × (CC − PICt−1). "This year's call = the call rate × the commitment not yet paid in." PIC is paid-in capital to date.

2. Distribution rate: RDt = max[ Y , (t ÷ L)B ]. "The share of NAV paid out rises with the fund's age; a bigger bow B keeps it low for longer." Y is a floor for income-paying assets.

3. Distributions: Dt = RDt × NAVt−1 × (1 + G). "Pay out that share of last year's NAV after this year's growth."

4. NAV: NAVt = NAVt−1 × (1 + G) + Ct − Dt. "Grow it, add what came in, take off what went out."

The six inputs are from the source [S454] CONFIRMED; this written-out form is the widely used textbook version INFERRED.

Worked example 4: one £100m commitment, step by step (illustrative numbers)

Inputs: CC = £100m; L = 10 years; B = 2.5; G = 10% a year; Y = 0; call rates RC = 25% in year 1, 33.3% in year 2, then 50% of whatever remains each year after.

YearCall CtNAV after growthRDt = (t/10)2.5Distribution DtNAVtPaid in to date

Run the same four equations to year 10 and the fund pays back £148.5m in total, 1.49 times the money paid in. But look at the NAV column: it never reaches £100m. It peaks at £91.0m in year 4 and then shrinks, because distributions start before the last calls arrive. A client that wants £100m of exposure cannot get it by committing £100m once.

Worked example 5: a five-year pacing plan (illustrative numbers)

A client with a £10bn portfolio wants 10% (£1.0bn) in private equity NAV. It has none today. Assume the portfolio stays at £10bn and every commitment behaves like the fund above. How much should it commit each year? Try £300m a year and run the model for each vintage (a "vintage" is the year a fund starts investing).

How each cell is built: year 3's NAV of £469.2m is three vintages added together: vintage 1 at age 3 (3 × 79.4 = 238.2), vintage 2 at age 2 (3 × 52.0 = 156.0) and vintage 3 at age 1 (3 × 25.0 = 75.0). Calls and distributions are summed the same way. All figures £m, illustrative.

Over-commitment and the commitment ratio

Look at year 5 of the plan: the client has signed £1.5bn of commitments to hold £1.0bn of NAV, and £506m is still uncalled. Over-commitment is the deliberate practice of committing more than the NAV target, because some of the money will not be called for years and some will already have come back INFERRED. Two ratios keep it honest:

  • Commitment ratio = total commitments ÷ target NAV = 1,500 ÷ 1,000 = 1.5x in the plan.
  • Unfunded cover = liquid assets ÷ unfunded commitments. The unfunded £506m is a real promise: if all of it were called in a bad year, the client must pay from its liquid assets. A client with £2bn of cash and bonds has cover of about 4x.

The steady-state check uses KKR's own figure. If distributions run at 10% to 30% of NAV a year [S436] CONFIRMED, a £1.0bn private book returns roughly £100m to £300m a year. To hold NAV flat, new calls must replace that, which is why mature programmes commit every year rather than in bursts INFERRED. KKR's TPA paper argues for "steadier commitment pacing" and "better vintage diversification" for exactly this reason [S435] CONFIRMED.

Worked example 6: the denominator effect, now with pacing (illustrative numbers)

Part 1 showed the basic denominator effect. Here it is with the pacing plan's year-5 numbers, so you can see what it does to next year's commitment.

The client now has three choices, each with a cost. (1) Pause commitments. Cheap today, but it leaves a hole in the vintage sequence that shows up as missing NAV five years later. (2) Sell fund stakes in the secondary market. Evercore reported high-quality buyout interests clearing at around 90% of NAV in H1 2026 [S267] CONFIRMED; selling £200m at 90% crystallises a £20m loss. (3) Widen the lens. KKR's TPA paper models a hypothetical 40% public equity drawdown that mechanically makes private assets look overweight, and argues a total portfolio risk budget gives "greater flexibility through drawdowns and denominator effects" [S435] CONFIRMED. This is not a textbook problem: S&P Global found 174 of 298 pension funds over-allocated to private equity in Q1 2025 [S324] REPORTED, and 53% of LPs in an ILPA survey said undrawn commitments limit new private equity commitments [S262] CONFIRMED.

What you will learn

To run the Takahashi-Alexander model by hand, turn a NAV target into a yearly commitment budget, size over-commitment and unfunded cover, and predict when the denominator effect will freeze a client's next commitment.

Where you meet it at KKR

Every flagship fundraise depends on clients' pacing budgets: North America Fund XIV closed at about $23bn in April 2026 [S459] REPORTED and Global Infrastructure Investors V at $19.2bn in August 2026 [S430] CONFIRMED. KKR's TPA papers name "pacing and recycled distributions" as a core private markets tool [S436] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

KKR raises drawdown funds and evergreen funds in the same asset classes, so a KCS team can offer a client two routes to the same exposure: a paced commitment programme, or an evergreen vehicle such as K-INFRA that is invested from the day money arrives (about 85% infrastructure assets, 15% liquidity portfolio) [S421] CONFIRMED. Learning when each route wins needs a shelf with both, which few managers have at KKR's scale INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn commitment pacing properly, the Yale model, over-commitment and the denominator effect, because a client's 'yes' to a KKR fund is really a yes to a five-year cash flow plan."

Reading performance like an LP@@l3-performance@@

A KCS team spends much of its life presenting performance, and clients' due diligence teams spend theirs taking it apart. ILPA's due diligence questionnaire has a whole section on track record [S444] CONFIRMED, and ILPA's 2025 Performance Template standardises IRR and TVPI/MOIC, gross and net, "with and without the impact of fund-level subscription facilities" [S445] CONFIRMED. This section teaches each measure, then shows how each one can flatter or mislead.

IRR: the return that cares about timing

The internal rate of return is the single annual interest rate that makes all of a fund's cash flows, in and out, worth exactly zero today.

Formula

0 = Σ CFt ÷ (1 + IRR)t. In words: discount every cash flow back to day 0 at the rate IRR; IRR is the rate at which the money you put in and the money you got out cancel exactly. Calls are negative CF, distributions positive, and any remaining NAV is treated as a final positive flow.

Worked example 7 (illustrative). An LP pays £100m at year 0 and £50m at year 1, receives £60m at year 2 and £150m at year 4. There is no algebraic shortcut, so you try rates:

  • At 10%: −100 − 50 ÷ 1.10 + 60 ÷ 1.102 + 150 ÷ 1.104 = −100 − 45.45 + 49.59 + 102.45 = +6.59. Positive, so the true rate is higher.
  • At 15%: −100 − 43.48 + 45.37 + 85.76 = −12.35. Negative, so the rate is lower.
  • Straight-line between them: 10% + 5% × 6.59 ÷ (6.59 + 12.35) = 11.7%. A spreadsheet's IRR function gives 11.6%.

The timing trap

IRR rewards speed, as the figure after the multiples below shows. Return money early and IRR jumps even if total profit is small. Delay the call with a credit line and IRR jumps again even though the LP pays interest. Neither makes the LP richer. So an LP never reads IRR alone: it reads it next to the multiples below INFERRED.

The multiples: TVPI, DPI, RVPI and MOIC

Formulas

DPI (distributions to paid-in) = cumulative distributions ÷ paid-in capital. "How much cash have I actually had back?" RVPI (residual value to paid-in) = current NAV ÷ paid-in. "How much is still on paper?" TVPI (total value to paid-in) = DPI + RVPI. MOIC (multiple on invested capital) is the same idea measured on the money put into deals, usually gross of fees, often deal by deal (standard industry definitions) INFERRED.

Worked example 8. Take the fund from the pacing section at the end of year 6: paid in £96.9m, distributed £57.0m to date, NAV £73.4m. DPI = 57.0 ÷ 96.9 = 0.59x. RVPI = 73.4 ÷ 96.9 = 0.76x. TVPI = 0.59 + 0.76 = 1.35x. In words: the LP has had 59p back for every £1 paid in, and the manager values what is left at another 76p.

Gross versus net: the fee drag, worked

Gross returns are what the deals earned. Net returns are what the LP kept after management fees, fund expenses and carried interest. The gap is the price of the manager. Drawdown funds traditionally charge a management fee of about 1.5% to 2% a year and carried interest of about 20% above a hurdle, often 8% (standard industry terms; exact terms vary by fund) INFERRED. KKR's evergreen K-PEC uses a different formula: 1.25% a year of NAV and 15% of total return above a 5% hurdle, with a high-water mark and a 100% catch-up [S420] CONFIRMED.

Worked example 9 (illustrative, simplified). An LP commits and pays in £100m over the fund's life. Management fees take £12m in total, so £88m goes into deals. The deals return 2.2 times their cost. Assume a single 5-year holding period, the 8% hurdle is cleared, and a full catch-up gives the manager 20% of all profit.

  1. Gross value = 88 × 2.2 = £193.6m. Gross MOIC = 2.20x.
  2. Profit over everything the LP paid = 193.6 − 100 = £93.6m. Hurdle check: 100 × 1.085 = 146.9 < 193.6, so carry is due.
  3. Carry = 20% × 93.6 = £18.7m. LP receives 193.6 − 18.7 = £174.9m. Net TVPI = 1.75x.
  4. Over five years: gross IRR = 2.201/5 − 1 = 17.1%; net IRR = 1.7491/5 − 1 = 11.8%. Fee drag = 5.3 points a year.

PME: did the fund beat the stock market?

An 11% IRR is good or bad only relative to what the same money would have made in listed shares. Kaplan and Schoar's public market equivalent (2003 working paper, 2005 Journal of Finance) answers that. In their words: "The PME measures the discounted value of the cash outflows of the fund relative to the discounted value of the cash inflows (all net of fees), where discounting is undertaken using the total return to the S&P 500" [S453] CONFIRMED. Above 1.0 means the fund beat the index INFERRED. Their research found average net returns roughly equal to the S&P 500, with large dispersion and strong persistence between a manager's successive funds [S453] REPORTED.

Formula

KS-PME = [ Σ Dt ÷ It ] ÷ [ Σ Ct ÷ It ]. In words: convert every distribution and every call into "units of the index" by dividing by the index level on that date, then compare units out with units in. (Remaining NAV counts as a final distribution.)

Vintage years and quartiles

A fund's vintage is the year it started investing. Funds of the same vintage bought in the same market, so they are ranked against each other, not against funds from other years. A quartile splits that peer group into four: "top quartile" means the best 25% of funds of that vintage on a chosen measure (net IRR, TVPI or PME). Two traps follow. First, the same number can rank very differently in two vintages. Second, a young fund's quartile is mostly RVPI, so it can move a lot before the fund is finished INFERRED.

Why DPI dominates in 2025-26

For a decade, LPs ranked managers mainly on IRR and TVPI. Since 2022 exits have slowed, cash has not come back, and DPI has become the most-watched number [S262] CONFIRMED. The evidence, provider by provider (each defines the base differently, so do not add them up):

  • Bain: distributions as a share of NAV were about 14% in 2025, below 15% for four years running [S262] CONFIRMED.
  • McKinsey's 2026 report, via Dakota: distributions fell to 6% of AUM versus a 2015 to 2019 average of 16% [S261] REPORTED.
  • PitchBook: almost $5 trillion sits in funds 7 or more years old, 39% of total NAV (December 2025) [S260] REPORTED. Bain counts about 32,000 unsold portfolio companies worth about $3.8tn [S262, S326] CONFIRMED.
  • Bain cites an ILPA survey: 53% of LPs said undrawn commitments limit new PE commitments [S262] CONFIRMED.

What this means at KKR: an exit is now a client-service event as much as an investment one. When Aon agreed to buy USI Insurance Services from KKR and co-investors for about $17bn including debt in August 2026, coverage put the price at about 6x KKR's original equity [S354] REPORTED. A realisation like that turns RVPI into DPI for every LP in the fund, and becomes evidence in the next fundraise INFERRED. The KCS question to ask is not "what is the IRR?" but "how much of the TVPI is cash?"

What you will learn

To compute IRR by trial and error and spot timing and credit-line effects; to split TVPI into DPI and RVPI; to walk gross to net through fees and carry; to compute a Kaplan-Schoar PME; and to rank a fund correctly within its vintage.

Where you meet it at KKR

ILPA DDQ 2.0 sections 14 (Track Record) and 15 (Accounting/Valuation) [S444] CONFIRMED; the ILPA Performance Template for funds starting from 1 January 2026, with first delivery four full quarters later [S445] CONFIRMED; and every fundraise update after an exit such as USI [S354] REPORTED.

Only at KKR

KKR reports performance for both drawdown funds (IRR, multiples) and evergreen K-Series vehicles (total returns by share class) [S420, S421] CONFIRMED. A KCS junior has to explain both families to the same clients, sometimes in the same meeting. That pushes you to understand what each number measures, not just to quote it INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn to read a track record the way an LP's due diligence team does: IRR next to DPI, gross next to net, and every number against a public market equivalent."

Evergreen performance: a different clock@@l3-evergreen@@

KKR's K-Series funds have no end date. Investors buy in monthly at NAV and can ask to sell quarterly, up to a cap (5% of NAV per quarter for K-PEC) [S420] CONFIRMED. There are no capital calls and no final distribution, so IRR and DPI do not fit. Evergreen funds report a total return per share class instead.

Three formulas

Total return for a period = (NAV per share at end + distributions paid − NAV per share at start) ÷ NAV per share at start.

Chain-linking: a yearly return is built from monthly ones: (1 + rJan) × (1 + rFeb) × ... × (1 + rDec) − 1. This is a time-weighted return: it ignores when investors added or withdrew money, so it measures the manager, not the investor's timing.

Distribution rate = annualised distribution per share ÷ current NAV per share. It is a cash yield, not a return.

Worked example 10 (illustrative). Three months of NAV moves: +1.0%, −0.5%, +2.0%. Quarter return = 1.010 × 0.995 × 1.020 − 1 = 2.505%, slightly more than the 2.5% you get by adding; over twelve months the gap grows. Now an infrastructure-style share class: NAV starts the year at £25.00, pays £0.30 a quarter, ends at £24.60. Distribution rate = (4 × 0.30) ÷ 24.60 = 4.9%. Total return = (24.60 + 1.20 − 25.00) ÷ 25.00 = 3.2%. A 4.9% payout on a 3.2% return means part of the cash came from capital. K-INFRA's own 10-K warns that its distributions "may and are expected to exceed our earnings and cash flow from operating activities" and may be paid from borrowings or offering proceeds [S421] CONFIRMED.

The real numbers: K-PEC and K-INFRA in 2025

Why do classes of the same fund earn different returns? Mainly fees. Classes S and U pay an ongoing 0.85% of NAV a year to the dealer-manager (0.60% distribution plus 0.25% servicing), class D pays 0.25%, and class I pays none [S420] CONFIRMED. The I-minus-U gap in K-PEC is 16.81 − 15.78 = 1.03 points, close to the 0.85% trail plus its knock-on effect on the performance fee INFERRED. The I-minus-D gap (2.01 points) is larger than D's 0.25% trail can explain; the timing of when D shares were first issued may matter, and the 10-K would need reading class by class INFERRED. Lesson: always quote the class.

Why evergreen returns and drawdown IRRs are apples and oranges

A drawdown fund's IRR is money-weighted: it measures only the money the fund has called, while it is invested. An evergreen total return is time-weighted and applies to all the money from day one, including a liquidity sleeve held in cash and credit (up to about 20% for K-PEC, about 15% for K-INFRA) [S420, S421] CONFIRMED. A drawdown LP's uncalled commitment sits elsewhere earning less, and the IRR ignores that.

Worked example 11 (illustrative). The same assets grow 10% a year. Investor 1 commits £100 to the drawdown fund from the pacing section and holds the uncalled money and distributions in cash at 4%. Investor 2 puts £100 into an evergreen fund holding the same assets. After five years:

  • Drawdown: NAV £88.6 + cash £48.5 = £137.1. The fund's IRR on called capital is a perfect 10.0%, but the committed £100 grew only 6.5% a year.
  • Evergreen, fully invested: 100 × 1.105 = £161.1 (10.0% a year).
  • Evergreen with a 15% liquidity sleeve at 4%: 100 × (0.85 × 1.105 + 0.15 × 1.045) = £155.1 (9.2% a year).

Four more differences to name when a client compares the two: fees are charged on NAV in evergreen funds but often on commitments in drawdown funds (K-PEC [S420] CONFIRMED; the drawdown norm INFERRED); evergreen NAVs are set monthly by the manager under board-approved policies, which is why operational due diligence focuses on valuation governance "where transactions are based on the fund's NAV" [S458] CONFIRMED; liquidity is real but capped, and in Q1 2026 K-FIT received requests for 6.3% of shares and paid about 80% of them, then paid in full in Q2 [S338] REPORTED; and evergreen investors who join late buy at today's NAV, so they do not get the early J-curve but also do not get the early discount INFERRED.

What you will learn

To compute time-weighted total returns and distribution rates, to read share-class returns net of trail fees, and to explain to a client why a 10% IRR and a 10% evergreen return do not deliver the same wealth.

Where you meet it at KKR

K-Series AUM reached $42bn at 30 June 2026, up from $25bn a year earlier [S330, S332] CONFIRMED. K-PEC and K-INFRA publish class-by-class returns in their 10-Ks [S420, S421] CONFIRMED. In London, Matt Yates is responsible for "ongoing product management of KKR's Evergreen Private Equity vehicles, K-PRIME and K-PEC" [S133] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

KKR's evergreen vehicles hold the same deals as its flagships through joint ventures: K-PEC is co-general partner alongside funds such as North America Fund XIII or XIV [S420] CONFIRMED. So a KCS junior can compare a drawdown fund and an evergreen fund that own overlapping companies, and see the measurement difference cleanly, without the noise of different portfolios INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn to explain evergreen and drawdown performance on a like-for-like basis, so a client comparing a K-Series return with a flagship IRR understands what each number actually measures."

Insurance clients: capital, matching and Global Atlantic@@l3-insurance@@

Insurers are a third of KKR's AUM when Global Atlantic's balance sheet (22%) and third-party insurers (11%) are added together (31 Dec 2025) [S71] CONFIRMED; the sum is INFERRED. They think about investments differently from anyone else, because every asset uses up regulatory capital.

How a life insurer makes money, from zero

A customer pays a lump sum for an annuity: a promise of fixed payments for years. The insurer invests the money in bonds and loans. Its profit is the spread: asset yield minus the rate it credits to the customer minus costs. Three constraints bind every investment decision INFERRED:

  1. Matching. Asset cash flows should arrive when the annuity payments are due (asset-liability management, ALM). Self-amortising assets help: KKR describes asset-based finance (ABF) investors receiving "front-loaded principal payments plus interest, similar to the way homeowners make their mortgage payments" [S434] CONFIRMED.
  2. Capital. US life insurers hold risk-based capital (RBC) against each asset. "The determination of RBC is based on the NAIC designation of the assets"; and the designation "depends on the applicable NRSRO rating" (a recognised credit rating) [S425] CONFIRMED. The NAIC moved from 6 to 20 bond designation categories with year-end 2021 factors [S461] REPORTED.
  3. Liquidity. Enough to pay claims and surrenders without forced selling INFERRED.

So an insurer wants assets that are investment-grade rated (low capital charge) but pay more than public bonds with the same rating. Private investment-grade credit and ABF are built for that: the capital rule is from [S425] CONFIRMED, the preference is INFERRED.

Worked example 12: capital charges with real NAIC numbers

KKR's digest of the NAIC's Spring 2026 meeting (Andrew Phillips, 7 April 2026) gives these factors [S439] CONFIRMED. Multiply each by $100m to see the capital an insurer would hold:

Now the decision. Suppose (illustrative yields) a private ABF note designated in an A3-to-Baa3-type band pays 2.0% over the insurer's liability cost and needs about 2.7% capital, while a loan backed by a residual interest pays 4.0% over liability cost but needs 30% capital. Spread per unit of capital: 2.0 ÷ 2.7 = 0.74 versus 4.0 ÷ 30 = 0.13. The lower-yielding asset is more than five times better use of capital. This is why KKR's insurance content leads with capital, and why the NAIC's stated principle of "equal capital for equal risk" [S439] CONFIRMED matters commercially: each factor change re-ranks the menu INFERRED.

What insurers say they want: the 2026 KKR Insurance CIO Survey

Published 2 October 2026, the survey covers about 50 US insurers with nearly $2.6tn of AUM [S438] CONFIRMED. "Nearly 80% expect to maintain or accelerate their pace of private capital investment, while 91% plan to maintain or increase credit exposure", and "Every major Private Credit category is a net add in the survey, led by Asset-Based Finance" [S438] CONFIRMED. Their concerns: "Asset-liability matching, illiquidity limits, regulatory capital, reinvestment risk" [S438] CONFIRMED. The full deck is available via "your Relationship Manager", i.e. through KCS [S438] CONFIRMED. KKR's NAIC digest similarly ends with "reach out to your relationship manager or kkrinsurance@kkr.com" [S439] CONFIRMED: regulatory analysis is used as a client-coverage tool.

Global Atlantic: client, capital source and laboratory

Global Atlantic (GA) is KKR's retirement and life insurer, fully owned since KKR bought the remaining 37% for about $2.7bn, completed 2 January 2024 [S2, S11] CONFIRMED. At 30 June 2026 it had $220bn of AUM, of which $164bn was credit [S1] CONFIRMED. For KCS it plays three roles at once [S425, S440] INFERRED:

  • A client of KKR's investing teams: GA's balance sheet is 22% of KKR's AUM [S71] CONFIRMED.
  • A source of products KCS sells: GA-sponsored sidecars raise third-party capital (below), and GA generates capital markets fees [S440] CONFIRMED. CFO Robert Lewin described the Arctos acquisition as a way to "create flow for Global Atlantic" [S440] CONFIRMED.
  • A source of insight for insurer clients: KKR's Global Insurance Client Solutions team offers "fully integrated balance sheet solutions" combining origination, capital markets access, "liability structuring solutions" and regulatory expertise [S104] CONFIRMED. Lewin on the cycle: in volatile markets "spreads are going to expand ... less competition on the liability side", and KKR has "real linkage between liability origination and asset origination" [S440] CONFIRMED.

The Ivy vehicles, explained simply

KKR's 10-K: "we also sponsor investment vehicles raised from third-party capital, such as the Ivy investment vehicles, to participate alongside Global Atlantic's institutional and individual market activities ... As of December 31, 2025, $58 billion of Global Atlantic AUM is provided by these Global Atlantic sponsored vehicles" [S425] CONFIRMED. They are "commitment-based structures with third-party investors" that participate "through reinsurance" in business GA writes during the vehicles' investment periods, and they are regulated by the Bermuda Monetary Authority [S425] CONFIRMED. By Q2 2026 IV-related AUM was "$62 billion ... up from approximately $50 billion just a year ago", with "$6 billion of dry powder that we think translates to north of $60 billion of buying power on the liability side" [S440] CONFIRMED.

The risk the investor takes is real: the 10-K notes a vehicle may "fail or refuse to fund their portion", and GA's remedy is against the vehicle, "but not directly against their shareholders or lenders" [S425] CONFIRMED. For KKR the economics are fees from "IV-related vehicles" plus GA-related capital markets fees "which we think can reach hundreds of millions annually over time"; total insurance economics were $2bn net of compensation over the last twelve months [S440] CONFIRMED.

Worked example 13: the sidecar's leverage (illustrative except the $6bn and $60bn)

KKR says $6bn of dry powder translates to "north of $60 billion of buying power on the liability side" [S440] CONFIRMED: about $1 of equity per $10 of liabilities. Suppose the assets backing those liabilities earn 1.0% a year more than the liabilities cost (illustrative). Each $1 of equity then earns $10 × 1.0% = $0.10 a year, about 10%, before costs and on top of whatever the equity's own assets earn. The same leverage cuts the other way: if the spread turned negative by 1.0%, the equity would lose about 10% a year. That is the underwriting risk the investor is paid for INFERRED.

What you will learn

To think like an insurance CIO: match liability cash flows, look up the capital charge, and rank assets by spread per unit of capital. To explain a reinsurance sidecar and its 10-to-1 leverage in plain words.

Where you meet it at KKR

Darryl Herrick, Head of Global Insurance Client Solutions and President of Global Atlantic Re, "helping manage the third-party reinsurance sidecar platform" [S127] CONFIRMED; Whitney Sparks's team raises "third-party capital to support KKR's Insurance business" [S128] CONFIRMED; the 2026 Insurance CIO Survey [S438] and NAIC updates [S439].

Only at KKR

Few managers own a large insurer outright, write its liabilities, originate the assets that back them, and raise third-party sidecar capital alongside it. At KKR a KCS insurance conversation can draw on all four. You learn insurance capital from the inside of a working insurer, not from a textbook [S425, S440] INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn why an insurer will take a lower yield for a lower capital charge, and how Global Atlantic and the Ivy vehicles turn that logic into products KCS raises money for."

Capstone: a private markets plan for a UK DC megafund@@l3-capstone@@

This exercise pulls the module together. It is the kind of analysis a London KCS team could prepare before meeting a large UK workplace pension. The scheme is hypothetical and all its numbers are illustrative; the rules and KKR facts are sourced.

Step 1: the client and its constraints

Scheme M (hypothetical) is a multi-employer defined contribution master trust. Its default fund is £30bn in 2027, growing to an assumed £40bn by 2030 from contributions and returns. Members are young on average, so contributions exceed withdrawals (illustrative). Its constraints:

  • Scale rule. Under the Pension Schemes Act 2026 (Royal Assent 29 April 2026), large multi-employer DC schemes need at least one default fund of £25bn or more by 2030, with a transition route for schemes of £10bn that can reach £25bn by 2035 [S346] CONFIRMED. Scheme M already qualifies.
  • Allocation pledge and reserve power. The voluntary Mansion House Accord (13 May 2025): 17 providers pledged at least 10% of DC default funds in private markets by 2030, with at least 5% in the UK [S315] CONFIRMED. The Act's reserve power, usable once and not before 1 January 2028, could require up to 10% in "qualifying assets", no more than 5% UK-specific, with a trustee opt-out where compliance is "likely not to be in the best interests of members" [S346] CONFIRMED.
  • Liquidity. The default fund is priced and dealt daily INFERRED. So private assets must sit in a vehicle that the daily fund can hold.
  • Vehicle rules. The UK Long-Term Asset Fund (LTAF) was opened to DC default and self-select use when PS23/7 recategorised LTAF units from 3 July 2023 [S305, S306] CONFIRMED. LTAFs deal no more often than monthly with at least 90 days' notice for redemptions [S451] REPORTED. LTAF AUM was about £7.5bn in May 2026 [S452] REPORTED.
  • Fees and value. DC fees are closely scrutinised, so low-fee routes (co-investment, SMAs, evergreen at scale) matter INFERRED.

Step 2: the allocation (illustrative)

Step 3: vehicle choice and pacing

KKR has no confirmed UK LTAF or EU ELTIF in the research [S429, S464] (gap), so the route has to be built INFERRED. A realistic structure: Scheme M (or its provider) runs its own LTAF; KKR manages a segregated mandate (a separately managed account) inside it, which commits to KKR drawdown funds and takes co-investments; a portion goes into semi-liquid or evergreen-style exposure that is invested from day one. KKR's 10-K notes institutions' "increased preference for ... separately managed accounts or specialized investment vehicles" [S70] CONFIRMED.

Pacing, worked. Split the 2030 target: 40% (£1.6bn) through evergreen-style exposure built by subscriptions, 60% (£2.4bn) through drawdown commitments. Using the single-commitment model from the pacing section, a commitment made each year from 2027 to 2030 will, at end-2030, be at ages 4, 3, 2 and 1, worth 91.0 + 79.4 + 52.0 + 25.0 = 247.4% of one year's commitment. Annual commitment needed = 2.4 ÷ 2.474 = about £0.97bn a year, so roughly £3.9bn committed in four years to hold £2.4bn of NAV, with about £1.6bn still uncalled (paid-in by then: 87.5% + 75% + 50% + 25% of one year's commitment). That is over-commitment in action (illustrative).

Module 2 in one paragraph

Liabilities set "enough". Each investment must beat what it replaces. Pacing turns NAV targets into commitments; watch the denominator. Read IRR with DPI, gross with net, against a PME and a vintage. Evergreen returns run on a different clock. Insurers rank assets by spread per unit of capital.

Part 4

Module 3: Learning a strategy well enough to explain it: infrastructure and asset-based credit

A KCS junior is not paid to know every asset. They are paid to explain one strategy so clearly that a pension trustee, an insurer and a private bank can each decide. This module takes the two strategies KKR is raising hardest in 2026, infrastructure and asset-based finance, and rebuilds them from the cash flows up.

Every claim below is tied to a source in the research files. Numbers marked "illustrative" are invented to teach a mechanism and are not KKR data. Where the research is silent, the text says so.

A1. What infrastructure is, technically@@l4-infra-what@@

What you will learn

To describe any infrastructure asset by five properties (how it is paid, how it tracks inflation, how long it lasts, how much capital it eats, and who carries the price risk), and to tell a concession from a merchant asset in one sentence.

KKR's research files describe infrastructure as "essential physical assets (power, data centres, roads, fibre)" that earn "long-term contracted or regulated cash flows" [S269] REPORTED. That phrase hides five separate technical properties. An LP (limited partner, the investor in a fund) will test each one.

PropertyPlain EnglishWhy an investor cares
1. Contracted or regulated revenueSomeone has agreed in advance what the asset will be paid: a regulator, a government, or one big customer (the "offtaker") on a long contract.Cash flows can be forecast years ahead, so the asset can carry more debt and the equity is less volatile INFERRED.
2. Inflation linkagePrices in the contract or regulated return rise with an inflation index, fully or partly.KKR's own 2026 mid-year outlook says inflation has a higher "resting heart rate" and portfolios need more links to nominal GDP [S360] CONFIRMED. Infrastructure is one answer INFERRED.
3. Long asset lifeA wind farm, fibre network or pipeline works for decades.Long life matches long liabilities (pensions, annuities). It also means the exit price depends on cash flows far in the future INFERRED.
4. Capex intensityCapex (capital expenditure) is money spent building or upgrading the asset. Infrastructure needs a lot of it up front.Norfolk Vanguard needs "more than $15bn" of total spending before it operates [S357] REPORTED. Whoever funds capex earns the return, which is why KKR is invited in INFERRED.
5. Who carries price riskEither the contract fixes the price (concession, contracted, regulated) or the asset sells at the market price (merchant).This one property decides most of the risk and return. Figure below.
Key terms

Concession: a government grants a company the right to build or run an asset (a toll road, an airport) for a fixed number of years, then the asset goes back to the state. Merchant: the asset sells its output (electricity, capacity) at whatever the market pays that day, with no long contract. Offtaker: the customer who buys the output under a contract. Contract for Difference (CfD): a UK government contract that tops up a power generator's income when the market price is below an agreed "strike price" and takes back the excess when the market is above it. The CfD explanation is a general description INFERRED; that Norfolk Vanguard won CfDs is [S357] REPORTED.

KKR describes what its European infrastructure team buys in exactly these terms: "digital infrastructure (fibre, data centres, towers), energy transition (wind, solar), transport", on a platform launched in 2008 [S192] CONFIRMED. The open-ended core fund's sector list, from a 2021 consultant memo, adds the regulated end: "water, wastewater, electricity and gas distribution, digital, renewables, contracted transport, energy transition", OECD countries only [S432] CONFIRMED.

Where you meet it at KKR

In London KCS, four people work on infrastructure product: Rory Smith, James Hewitt (infrastructure and Climate Transition), Larisa Svintsitski and Pablo Arribas (infrastructure for individuals) [S150] CONFIRMED. It is the largest product cluster in London KCS [S150] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

A textbook teaches the four revenue models. At KKR you would see one team own a CfD wind farm (Norfolk Vanguard), contracted data centres (Global Technical Realty) and a fibre network (FiberCop) at once [S181, S182, S430] CONFIRMED, so you learn to compare them inside one portfolio rather than one at a time INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn to read an infrastructure asset by who carries the price risk, because that single fact explains why a CfD wind farm and a merchant power plant sit at opposite ends of the same fund."

A2. The risk ladder: core, core-plus, value-add, and KKR's vehicles on it@@l4-ladder@@

What you will learn

The industry's four-rung ladder for infrastructure risk, and how KKR uses different vehicles (an open-ended core fund, a drawdown flagship, a climate fund and an evergreen wealth vehicle) to sell different rungs to different clients.

Infrastructure managers sort strategies by how much of the return comes from steady cash today versus growth and building tomorrow. Core means operating assets with contracted or regulated income: most of the return is cash yield. Core-plus adds some growth (expansion, re-contracting, bolt-ons). Value-add takes construction, repositioning or platform-building risk. Opportunistic or development means building new assets or businesses where most of the return is capital gain. These labels are industry convention INFERRED; KKR's own documents use two of them directly.

  • DCIF (Diversified Core Infrastructure Fund). Open-ended (no end date), started December 2020 [S175] CONFIRMED. The consultant memo calls it "an open-ended fund that will focus on critical core infrastructure investments with low volatility and strong downside protection", targeting 7% to 9% net [S432] CONFIRMED.
  • GII V. A drawdown fund (investors commit and KKR calls the money over time), investing July 2024 to July 2030 [S1] CONFIRMED. KKR labels it "Core+" [S430] CONFIRMED, not value-add. A flagship drawdown fund takes more growth and development risk than DCIF, so it sits a rung or more higher INFERRED.
  • Global Climate Transition Fund. Started July 2024 [S175] CONFIRMED; James Hewitt's London KCS role covers "Climate Transition" [S150] CONFIRMED. Its return target is not in the research [Gap].
  • K-INFRA. The evergreen vehicle for individuals. It owns slices of assets through joint ventures with the other funds [S421] CONFIRMED, so it spans the ladder. About 85% infrastructure, about 15% liquid assets, at most 15% outside the OECD [S421] CONFIRMED.
  • Helix Digital Infrastructure. Launched June 2026 for AI data centres, power and connectivity for hyperscalers (the largest cloud companies), led by former AWS CEO Adam Selipsky with NVIDIA and Vistra as partners [S3] CONFIRMED. A new platform sits at the development end INFERRED.

Same team, different wrappers: what changes for the client

DCIF (institutional, open-ended)GII V (institutional, drawdown)K-INFRA (individuals, evergreen)

That choice tells the client the manager is paid to deliver steady distributions, which is what a core investor wants [S432] INFERRED. The memo is from 2021 and the terms may have changed [S432]; it also contains factual slips (it says KKR was "founded in 1996"; KKR was founded in 1976), a reminder that consultant documents need checking [S432] CONFIRMED.

Where you meet it at KKR

K-INFRA's 10-K lists every fund it co-invests with [S421]. When a private bank asks "is this the same as the institutional fund?", the London KCS answer (Pablo Arribas covers infrastructure for individuals [S150]) is: same assets, alongside those funds, different wrapper, fees and liquidity INFERRED.

Only at KKR

Few managers sell one infrastructure team through a core open-ended fund, a flagship drawdown fund, a climate fund, an evergreen wealth vehicle and a new AI platform at once [S175, S421, S3]. Learning which client belongs on which rung is a skill you can only practise where all the rungs exist INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to understand why KKR sells the same infrastructure team as DCIF at 0.8% with carry on cash yield, and as K-INFRA at 1.25% with 12.5% over a 5% hurdle, and which client each one is built for."

A3. KKR's themes and the European deals behind them@@l4-themes@@

What you will learn

To connect a macro theme to a fund to a named asset in three steps, the chain every infrastructure pitch is built on.

KKR's 2026 mid-year outlook calls "Security of Everything" a capital allocation decision covering "defence, cyber, data, shipping lanes, food, water, energy and critical minerals", and in Europe favours "infrastructure, defence, grids, energy systems, logistics" [S360] CONFIRMED. CFO Robert Lewin named "AI, digital and energy infrastructure, defence" among four growth drivers [S3] CONFIRMED. Infrastructure has doubled since KKR's April 2024 Investor Day [S3] CONFIRMED. KKR now manages about $120bn of infrastructure equity, "up from just $13 billion in 2019", with about 160 dedicated infrastructure executives and more than 100 investments [S430] CONFIRMED.

A4. How an infrastructure asset makes money: cash yield versus total return@@l4-money@@

What you will learn

To build an equity cash flow from revenue down (the "waterfall"), to compute cash yield and debt service cover, and to split a total return into the part paid in cash and the part paid at exit.

Step 1: the cash-flow anatomy of a wind farm

Every infrastructure asset is valued from one ladder of deductions. Start with revenue. Take off operating costs to reach EBITDA (earnings before interest, tax, depreciation and amortisation: the cash the asset makes from running). Take off tax and maintenance capex to reach CFADS (cash flow available for debt service). Pay the lenders interest and scheduled principal. What remains belongs to equity. Lenders watch the DSCR (debt service cover ratio):

DSCR = CFADS ÷ (interest + scheduled principal)

A DSCR of 1.38x means the asset earns 38% more cash than it needs to pay its lenders that year. If DSCR falls below a level set in the loan, cash is "locked up" and equity gets nothing until it recovers. This is general project finance practice INFERRED.

Step 2: worked example, cash yield versus total return (illustrative numbers)

Worked example: a contracted asset held for ten years (illustrative numbers)

Buy. Enterprise value £1,200m. Debt £720m (60%) at 5.5%, repaid £20m a year. Equity cheque £480m.

Year 1. EBITDA £90m. Tax and maintenance capex £15m. Interest 5.5% × £720m = £39.6m. Principal £20m. Cash to equity = 90 - 15 - 39.6 - 20 = £15.4m.

Cash yield = cash to equity ÷ equity invested = 15.4 ÷ 480 = 3.2%.

Years 2 to 10. The contract is indexed, so EBITDA and costs grow 2.5% a year. Debt falls, so interest falls. By year 10 cash to equity is £44.0m, a 9.2% yield on the original cheque. Average over ten years: 6.1%.

Exit. Year 11 EBITDA £115.2m × 12 (the same multiple as entry, rounded down slightly) = £1,382m. Less remaining debt £520m = £862m to equity.

Total return. The internal rate of return (IRR: the single annual rate that makes all cash in equal all cash out in today's money) on -480 then the yearly cash then +862 is 10.5%. If the exit only returned the £480m cheque, the IRR would be 5.8%. So about 5.8 points come from cash and about 4.7 from growth plus debt paydown.

This split explains real KKR documents. K-INFRA's 10-K warns that distributions "may and are expected to exceed our earnings and cash flow from operating activities" [S421] CONFIRMED. In the example, early-year cash yield is low (3.2%) while value is building, so a vehicle promising a steady payout must sometimes pay out more than the assets yield that year INFERRED. And DCIF's carry on "asset level cash yield" above 4% [S432] pays the manager only for the blue part of the bar INFERRED.

Step 3: the data centre version of the same story

That curve is why a fund like GII V and a platform like Helix can own the same kind of asset and still be different products. The investor who funds the trough earns the climb; the investor who buys at the stabilised stage earns the yield INFERRED. KKR's earlier $50bn partnership with Energy Capital Partners for data centres and power generation [S35] REPORTED and Helix's focus on "power and connectivity" [S3] CONFIRMED show that power supply, not buildings, is now the binding constraint KKR talks about INFERRED.

Where you meet it at KKR

GII V had already committed over $9bn at close, including Global Technical Realty and Gulf Data Hub [S430] CONFIRMED. An LP update on GTR's extra $1.5bn equity [S182] uses exactly this vocabulary INFERRED.

Only at KKR

KKR's insurance balance sheet bought the TotalEnergies stake [S180] while DCIF pays carry on cash yield [S432]. Seeing which buyer prices the blue bar and which prices the green bar, inside one firm, is the lesson INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn to split an infrastructure return into cash yield and growth, because that split decides whether an asset belongs in a core fund, in GII V or in K-INFRA."

B1. Asset-based finance: lending against pools, not companies@@l4-abf@@

What you will learn

What a pool of loans or receivables is, how it is cut into tranches, why amortising cash flows differ from a direct loan, and how to compute who loses what when defaults rise.

KKR defines asset-based finance (ABF) as "private credit investments backed by large, diversified pools of hard or financial assets", and adds that "private ABF is considered non-corporate credit" [S434] CONFIRMED. Translation: you do not lend to one company and hope it survives. You lend against thousands of small loans or leases (car loans, aircraft leases, royalties, development loans) whose combined repayments pay you back INFERRED. K-FIT's 10-K gives KKR's own examples: "auto loans, royalties, aircraft leasing, real estate development lending" [S422] CONFIRMED.

Direct lendingAsset-based finance
BorrowerOne company, usually owned by a PE fundA pool of many loans or assets, held in a special purpose vehicle (SPV)
Paid back fromThe company's profitsRepayments on the pool, plus the value of the collateral
Cash flow shape"interest-only coupons" with principal at the end [S434]"front-loaded principal payments plus interest, similar to the way homeowners make their mortgage payments" [S434]
Main riskOne company failsMany small borrowers default together; servicer fails
KKR scale$48bn corporate private credit, of which $39bn direct lending (30 Jun 2026) [S332]$91bn ABF AUM (30 Jun 2026) [S332]

Worked example: a $100m auto-loan pool, tranched (illustrative numbers)

Worked example: who gets paid, who loses (illustrative numbers)

The pool. $100m of car loans yielding 11% a year. Expected annual losses 2.5%. Servicing (collecting payments, chasing arrears) costs 1.5%. Net pool yield = 11 - 2.5 - 1.5 = 7.0%, or $7.0m in year one.

The tranches. Senior $80m paid 5.5% ($4.40m). Mezzanine $10m paid 8.5% ($0.85m). Equity (first loss) $10m gets whatever is left: 7.0 - 4.40 - 0.85 = $1.75m, a 17.5% return on $10m.

Stress. If losses rise to 5%, net yield is 4.5% ($4.5m). Senior still gets $4.40m. Mezzanine gets only $0.10m of its $0.85m. Equity gets nothing and starts absorbing principal losses: roughly -7.5% that year. The senior investor is untouched until losses eat through the $20m below it.

The formula in words. Credit enhancement for a tranche = the share of the pool below it. Senior here has 20% enhancement: pool losses must exceed 20% of principal before it loses a dollar.

KKR's market case: "We estimate the private ABF market today is larger than the syndicated loan, high yield bond, and direct lending markets combined, and it could reach approximately $9.2 trillion by 2029" (KKR and Integer Advisors data as of 31 March 2024) [S434] CONFIRMED. The reason it is private: "Post-crisis regulation and resulting higher capital requirements have reduced banks' risk appetites" [S434] CONFIRMED. KKR began ABF in 2016 [S433] REPORTED and closed Asset-Based Finance Partners II in July 2025 at $5.6bn plus nearly $1bn of separately managed accounts, $6.5bn in total, more than twice Fund I [S433] REPORTED. KKR's 2026 mid-year outlook says "collateral-based cash flows (asset-based finance, real estate credit, structured solutions) matter more" [S360] CONFIRMED.

Where you meet it at KKR

Credit KCS work is run largely from Dublin, a legacy of the 2014 Avoca purchase [S152] CONFIRMED. KKR also runs a registered interval fund called KKR Asset-Based Income Fund [S114] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

KKR owns many of the platforms that originate the loans (around 20, with 7,000+ staff [S3]), and owns an insurer that buys the senior paper [S425]. Seeing origination, structuring and the end buyer inside one firm is rare; most managers see one or two of the three INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn how KKR turns thousands of car loans or aircraft leases into a senior tranche an insurer can hold and a first-loss piece a fund wants, and who loses what when defaults rise."

B2. Why insurers want ABF, and how it reaches Core Plus+@@l4-insurers@@

What you will learn

The two reasons an insurer buys private credit (capital and matching), the regulatory factors that decide it, and the plumbing that puts KKR's private credit inside a Capital Group fund with a $1,000 minimum.

Reason 1: capital

A US life insurer must hold capital (risk-based capital, RBC) against each asset. KKR's 10-K says "the determination of RBC is based on the NAIC designation of the assets in which Global Atlantic invests", and the designation "depends on the applicable NRSRO rating" (a recognised credit rating agency) [S425] CONFIRMED. So an insurer wants assets that are rated investment grade, so the capital charge is low, but pay more than a public bond of the same rating INFERRED. A senior ABF tranche is built to be exactly that. KKR's own NAIC digest shows how much structure changes the charge:

Reason 2: matching

An annuity book pays policyholders a little every month for years. Assets that also pay back principal every month (the left shape in the ABF figure) match those outflows better than a bullet loan INFERRED. KKR's 2026 Insurance CIO Survey (about 50 US insurers, nearly $2.6tn of assets) lists insurers' concerns as "Asset-liability matching, illiquidity limits, regulatory capital, reinvestment risk", and says "Every major Private Credit category is a net add in the survey, led by Asset-Based Finance" [S438] CONFIRMED. Global Atlantic itself had $220bn of AUM at 30 June 2026, of which $164bn is credit and $62bn sits in Ivy and other sponsored reinsurance vehicles [S1] CONFIRMED.

Worked example: same rating, more spread, same capital (illustrative numbers)

An insurer holds $100m of A-rated assets. A public A-rated corporate bond pays 1.0% over government bonds. A senior ABF tranche, also rated A, pays 1.8% over (illustrative; the extra is payment for complexity and illiquidity). If the capital factor is the same for both ratings, the insurer earns an extra 0.8% × $100m = $0.8m a year for no extra capital. Across Global Atlantic's $164bn of credit [S1], every 0.1% of extra spread is about $164m a year INFERRED. That is why spreads, ratings and NAIC rules are KCS conversation topics with insurers.

The plumbing into Capital Group KKR Core Plus+

Core Plus+ is an interval fund (a fund that must offer to buy back a set share of its units every quarter) that allocates "approximately 60% of its net assets to public debt assets (to be managed by the investment adviser) and approximately 40% to private credit assets (to be managed by the sub-adviser)" [S426] CONFIRMED. The private credit is direct lending and asset-based finance [S92] CONFIRMED. KKR Credit Advisors (US) is the sub-adviser and is "paid solely by the investment adviser out of the investment adviser's fees" [S426] CONFIRMED.

Compare the promise with KKR's own evergreen funds. An interval fund must make the repurchase offer every quarter under a "fundamental policy" changeable only by shareholder vote; a K-Series board may repurchase less or nothing [S426, S420] CONFIRMED; the contrast is the author's INFERRED. Firmer liquidity requires more liquid assets, which is why the private sleeve is only about 40% INFERRED.

Where you meet it at KKR

Darryl Herrick heads Global Insurance Client Solutions and is President of Global Atlantic Re, "helping manage the third-party reinsurance sidecar platform" [S127] CONFIRMED. KKR's NAIC digest tells insurer clients to "reach out to your relationship manager" [S439] CONFIRMED. Daniel Celeghin leads New Markets and Sales Partnerships, including Capital Group [S136] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

KKR is both an insurer's asset manager and, through Global Atlantic, an insurer itself, with $58bn of third-party sidecar capital at end-2025 [S425]. You would learn insurance capital maths from people who live under the same NAIC rules as the client INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn why an insurer will pay for a senior ABF tranche, capital charge and cash-flow matching, and how the same KKR loans end up in a Capital Group fund with a $1,000 minimum."

C. Private equity briefly: one deal, a flagship and an evergreen owner@@l4-pe@@

What you will learn

How KKR splits one buyout between North America Fund XIV, the evergreen K-PEC and co-investors, and why the order of the split is the conflict KCS must explain to both sets of clients.

North America Fund XIV closed at about $23bn in April 2026, which KKR calls the largest private equity fund focused only on North America [S335] CONFIRMED; KKR's Q2 table shows $21.9bn committed, investing April 2025 to April 2031 [S1] CONFIRMED. The release reported a 23% gross IRR (19% net) across the three predecessor funds as of 31 December 2025 [S335] REPORTED.

K-PEC is built differently from a fund. It "seeks to acquire, own and control portfolio companies ... through Joint Ventures", and K-PEC and the flagship fund are "co-general partners" of each deal-level joint venture, with economic shares that "vary from acquisition to acquisition" [S420] CONFIRMED. Because its main asset is a general partner interest, which is not an "investment security", K-PEC keeps investment securities below the 40% test of the US Investment Company Act of 1940 and operates as a company, not a registered fund [S420] CONFIRMED.

Why the co-investment slice matters to the firm: since 2014 KKR's fund agreements share 100% of transaction and monitoring fees allocable to a fund with fund investors, while "transaction fees earned in our capital markets business are not shared with fund investors" [S425] CONFIRMED. KCM earned $930m of transaction fees in 2025 [S425] CONFIRMED. Co-investment that KCS places is therefore tied to a fee line that flagship LPs do not offset INFERRED. LPs know this: ILPA DDQ section 5 asks whether co-investments were "of a syndicated nature" and how "broken-deal expenses associated with co-investments are handled" [S444] CONFIRMED.

Where you meet it at KKR

Matt Yates in London is Head of EMEA Product Development and runs product management for K-PRIME and K-PEC [S150] CONFIRMED. K-PRIME is K-PEC's non-US twin with "similar objectives and strategies" [S111] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

K-PEC's co-GP joint venture is KKR's own legal answer to the 1940 Act [S420]; competitors use other routes INFERRED. Learning the allocation order from the 10-K that discloses it, and then explaining it to clients, is specific to this platform INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn how one KKR buyout is shared between North America Fund XIV, K-PEC and co-investors, and how to explain that order to a pension fund and a private bank without changing the story."

D1. How a KCS junior learns a strategy in a week@@l4-week@@

What you will learn

A repeatable reading path from house view to fund documents to client questions to track record, ending in a one-page brief you can defend.

Two technical tools from Friday's reading need defining. TVPI (total value to paid-in) is distributions plus remaining value divided by money paid in; DPI (distributions to paid-in) counts only cash actually returned. These are standard definitions INFERRED. The PME (public market equivalent) of Kaplan and Schoar "measures the discounted value of the cash outflows of the fund relative to the discounted value of the cash inflows (all net of fees), where discounting is undertaken using the total return to the S&P 500" [S453] CONFIRMED; above 1.0 means the fund beat the index INFERRED. The ILPA Performance Template, for funds starting on or after 1 January 2026, standardises IRR and TVPI "with and without the impact of fund-level subscription facilities" [S445] CONFIRMED. A subscription facility is a short-term loan a fund uses before calling LP money, which can flatter IRR INFERRED.

Where you meet it at KKR

KKR's TPA paper says that across private market strategies "annual distributions have historically averaged approximately 10-30% of NAV" [S436] CONFIRMED, and KKR's wealth model portfolios put 5% to 7.5% in infrastructure [S437] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

Few firms publish a 10-K for an evergreen infrastructure vehicle (K-INFRA), a consultant-reviewed core fund (DCIF) and a flagship close (GII V) on the same platform. A junior can cross-check the same assets in three public documents before opening the data room INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn KKR's way of mastering a strategy in a week, house view, documents, reports, DDQ and track record, and to finish with a one-page brief a relationship manager can use."
