@@l5@@
Part 5

Module 4: The machine room: co-investment, diligence, distribution rules and the tools

Behind every fund close sits a machine: deals syndicated through walls, questionnaires answered line by line, a rulebook applied to every email, and a record kept of all of it. This module takes the machine apart and shows each moving piece.

Parts 1 to 4 explained what KCS sells and why clients buy. This part explains how the work is actually done. It covers five mechanisms: co-investment syndication with KKR Capital Markets (KCM), the diligence process (DDQs, RFPs, operational due diligence, side letters), the distribution rules a junior applies every day, the tools and records, and a worked week-three task. Every KKR fact carries a source. Every number that is invented for teaching is labelled "illustrative".

Co-investment and syndication: how a deal is shared@@l5-coinvest@@

The idea in one paragraph

A co-investment is money a client puts directly into one specific deal, alongside a KKR fund, rather than into the fund itself. Syndication is the process of selling that slice of the deal to several clients. KKR describes the mechanism in its 10-K: its capital markets business "provides syndication services for co-investments in transactions participated in by KKR, our investment vehicles, Global Atlantic, and third-party clients, which may entitle the firm to receive transaction fees, management fees, and a carried interest" [S425] CONFIRMED. KCS's official team description gives it a role in exactly this: "Work in partnership with Capital Markets in the syndication/distribution of product" (KKR KCS team description) [Advert].

Key terms

Underwrite: to promise to provide the money first, then sell part of it later. KKR says: "We have used our balance sheet in our capital markets business to underwrite loans, securities or other financial instruments, which we generally expect to syndicate to third parties" [S425] CONFIRMED.
Sell-down: the amount the underwriter plans to sell on to others.
Co-underwriting: a client commits before the deal is signed and shares the underwriting risk with KKR. ILPA's due diligence questionnaire asks about it by name [S444] CONFIRMED.
Balance sheet: KKR's own money, as opposed to clients' money in funds.

The lifecycle, stage by stage

Figure 1 shows the six stages and who does what in each. Read it as four parallel lanes: the deal team with KCM, the KCS client team, Compliance, and the co-investor. Only the underwrite-then-syndicate logic, the risk of an unsold remainder, and the fee rules are KKR's own disclosures. The exact order of steps is a standard industry sequence that I have reconstructed INFERRED.

Three details in the lifecycle deserve attention, because they are where things go wrong.

  • The unsold remainder. KKR warns that "if we are unable to syndicate all or part of such commitment, or if such co-investors fail to fund their commitments, we may be required to fund the remaining commitment" [S70] CONFIRMED. KKR also uses "arrangements with third parties that reduce our risk associated with holding unsold securities ... which enables our capital markets business to underwrite a larger amount" [S425] CONFIRMED. So the speed and reliability of KCS's client list directly limits how big KKR can underwrite INFERRED.
  • Who gets asked first. Some clients hold co-investment rights in their side letters; others are simply fast. KKR names "increased co-investment rights" among the concessions it may grant in fundraising [S425] CONFIRMED. Allocation policy decides the order, and Compliance checks it INFERRED.
  • The new buyers. Evergreen vehicles now buy co-investments too. K-PEC, KKR's private equity vehicle for individuals, is offered a "first tier" alongside nearly all KKR vehicles "up to a capped amount", but "will not benefit from any priority acquisition allocation" [S420] CONFIRMED. Capital Group KKR U.S. Equity+ "expects to invest approximately 30% of its net assets in K-PEC and approximately 10% of its net assets in co-investments" [S427] CONFIRMED. A retail fund built by a third party is now one of the co-investors.

The economics: why KKR wants co-investment

This is the single most important technical fact in the module, and it comes straight from KKR's 10-K. There are two kinds of deal fee, and they are treated in opposite ways (Figure 2).

The exact words matter, so here they are. "Since 2014, our fund agreements typically require us to share 100% of any monitoring, transaction, and break-up fees that are allocable to a fund ... with fund investors, therefore these types of fees, when generated, are uniquely driven by co-investment opportunities." And: "transaction fees earned in our capital markets business are not shared with fund investors" [S425] CONFIRMED. KCM earned $930m of capital markets transaction fees in 2025 across 404 transactions (49 equity, 355 debt), up from 397 in 2024; fee rates are "generally higher" for equity than debt; about 15% of fees came from unaffiliated third parties [S425] CONFIRMED. In London, the KCM partnership (KKR Capital Markets Partners LLP) had an average of 10 employees and 10 members in 2025 and £244.2m of profit before members' remuneration [S172] CONFIRMED. A small team, a large number.

The formula, in words and symbols

KKR fee income from a deal = (fund-level fees × 0%) + (fees on co-investment capital) + (KCM capital markets fees).
In symbols: FKKR = 0 × Ffund + Fco + FKCM. The first term is zero because of the 100% offset [S425] CONFIRMED. The second and third terms grow with the amount of capital placed outside the fund INFERRED. One accounting detail: capital syndicated by KCM to third parties is excluded from KKR's "capital invested" metric [S425] CONFIRMED, so a large syndication can be invisible in that headline number.

What the client gains: the blended fee

For the client, co-investment is about cost. Fund investors pay a management fee and carried interest; co-investments often carry no fee or a low fee (Part 1 showed a blended fee rate; here we add carried interest and compute the effect on the client's net multiple). KKR's own 10-K gives the range for private equity drawdown funds: 1.0% to 2.0% of committed capital in the investment period [S2] CONFIRMED. The example below sits inside that range. Everything else is illustrative.

Illustrative numbers ($m)Fund onlyCo-invest, no feeBoth togetherCo-invest, low fee*
Capital invested1005015050
Gross profit at 2.0x1005015050
Management fee: 1.5% × 5 years7.507.51.25 (0.5%)
Carried interest: 20% × (profit minus fees)18.5018.54.9 (10%)
Total fees26.0026.06.1
Net profit74.050.0124.043.9
Net multiple (capital + net profit) ÷ capital1.74x2.00x1.83x1.88x
Fees as % of gross profit26.0%0%17.3%12.3%

*Low-fee variant: 0.5% a year and 10% carry, both illustrative. Simplifications: the fee is charged on the full $100m for five years; the hurdle is assumed cleared with full catch-up, so carry equals 20% of profit after fees. Standard 2-and-20 structure as described in [S2] (range) and WS4 (textbook terms) INFERRED.

The two formulas behind the chart: blended management fee = fund fee × F ÷ (F + C), and fees as a share of gross profit = 26% × F ÷ (F + C), where F is fund capital and C is co-investment capital. Both fall in a straight line as the co-invest share rises (Figure 3).

The risks a client must weigh

RiskWhat it meansHow it shows up at KKR
Adverse selectionThe fear that the manager offers co-investment on the deals it least wants to hold. The client gets the leftovers INFERRED.KKR's structure cuts both ways: flagship funds have a "mandatory minimum" filled first, and other vehicles take the rest [S420] CONFIRMED. Co-investment is the excess on deals the fund already backs at its maximum, not a deal the fund rejected INFERRED.
SpeedA co-investment decision may be needed in days or weeks. Many pensions need months and a board meeting INFERRED.Clients who can decide fast get asked first; clients who cannot need pre-approved programmes or co-invest funds INFERRED.
ConcentrationOne company, not a portfolio of 15 to 20 INFERRED.The client's own risk team sets a cap per deal; KCS logs it in the CRM INFERRED.
Broken-deal costsWho pays the costs of a deal that never happens.In 2015 the SEC found KKR misallocated more than $17m of broken-deal expenses to flagship funds rather than co-investors; KKR paid nearly $30m [S20] CONFIRMED. LPs still ask about it (next section).
Warehousing priceAn asset bought first by KKR and sold on later at cost may be worth more or less by then.K-INFRA's 10-K: transfers "at cost, or cost plus an interest rate or carrying cost", with conflicts that "may not be resolved in favor of the Company" [S421] CONFIRMED.

How LPs test it: ILPA DDQ section 5

The industry's standard questionnaire (next section) has a whole section on co-investment. Section 5 of ILPA DDQ 2.0 asks whether predecessor funds charged LP co-investors a transaction fee, management fee or carry; whether co-investments were "of a syndicated nature"; whether any "involved co-underwriting pre-signing"; and how "broken-deal expenses associated with co-investments are handled" [S444] CONFIRMED. Each of those questions maps onto a box in Figure 1. A junior who can explain why each one is asked has understood the whole lifecycle INFERRED.

Family Capital and "two-way sourcing"

KKR's Family Capital page lists "Co-Investment Opportunities", "Customized Portfolio Solutions", "Bespoke Separately Managed Accounts", "Two-Way Sourcing Dialogues" and "Comprehensive Debt Solutions" [S84] CONFIRMED. "Two-way" means the flow runs in both directions INFERRED:

  • KKR to family: co-investment in KKR deals, routed through the same syndication lifecycle.
  • Family to KKR: many wealthy families own operating businesses. They may bring KKR a deal, need financing for their own company, or want a partner. Scott Kaldenberg in Family Capital is "responsible for originating financing and capital markets opportunities across KKR's global platform, partnering closely with leading family offices and their operating companies" [S138] CONFIRMED. The France hire release says Family Capital "sources investment and capital markets opportunities" for families [S178] CONFIRMED.

The career paths show how close these teams are. Doug Brody, now Head of Family Capital for the Americas and EMEA, previously led "the Credit & Markets Co-investments and Partnerships team, which facilitates private capital investments and transactions across all of KKR's asset classes" [S125] CONFIRMED. Monica Mandelli sits on KKR's "Capital Markets Coordination Committee" [S134] CONFIRMED.

Information walls: inside information and wall-crossing, step by step@@l5-walls@@

Material non-public information (MNPI), often called inside information, is information that is not public and that a reasonable investor would use to decide whether to buy or sell a security. If a co-investment involves a listed company (a take-private, for example), the name of the target is MNPI until the deal is announced INFERRED. An information wall (or barrier) is the set of rules that keeps such information inside a small group. Wall-crossing is the controlled act of bringing an outsider, such as a client, inside that group, with their consent, so they can evaluate the deal INFERRED.

Why this lands on KCS: KCS works "in partnership with Capital Markets", and KCM handles financings and securities. KKR's UK capital markets entity is FCA-authorised for dealing and arranging [S70] CONFIRMED. Amy Lesch, KCS's Head of Global Product Strategy, is a member of KKR's "Global Conflicts and Compliance Committee" [S121] CONFIRMED. KKR's actual wall procedure is not public. Figure 4 shows the standard shape of a wall-crossing; treat it as a method to learn, not as KKR's manual.

The classic junior mistakes

Mentioning a live deal name to a client "to build excitement" before step 4. Forwarding a teaser from a personal phone. Adding a colleague to an email thread who is not on the wall. Each one breaks the wall even if nobody trades INFERRED. The off-channel settlement shows regulators enforce the record-keeping side too [S29] REPORTED.

What you will learn

The full syndication lifecycle from underwriting to reporting; the two-pipe fee rule and why it makes co-investment strategic for KKR; how to compute a client's blended fee and net multiple with and without co-investment; the five risks a co-investor weighs; and the seven-step wall-crossing that lets a client see a live deal lawfully.

Where you meet it at KKR

In Europe, Mark Danzey is both European Head of KKR Capital Markets and European Head of Institutional Client Solutions [S156] CONFIRMED. Co-investment demand is large across the market: PitchBook tracked $37.5bn of co-investment fundraising in the 12 months to June 2026 [S260] CONFIRMED. KKR's own macro team writes that portfolio exposures "inform future deployment decisions, including where to lean in and where to syndicate through co-investment" [S436] CONFIRMED.

Only at KKR

Other large managers run co-investment programmes, so the concept is not unique. What is unusual is the disclosure: KKR states in its 10-K that fund-level fees are 100% offset and that KCM fees are not [S425] CONFIRMED, so a junior can trace the economics of a syndication from a public document. Add one leader spanning KCM and institutional clients in Europe [S156], and the lifecycle is visible from both sides INFERRED.

Say it in one line

"I want to learn how KCS and KKR Capital Markets syndicate a co-investment, because it is where a client's blended fee falls and KKR's non-shared capital markets fees rise, so getting allocation right serves both."

The diligence machine: DDQs, RFPs and operational due diligence@@l5-ddq@@

Before a pension fund commits $200m, it asks hundreds of written questions. The answers become representations to the client: if one is wrong, it is a legal and regulatory risk INFERRED. Three document types carry most of this load.

DocumentWhat it isWho sends it, who answers
DDQ (due diligence questionnaire)A long, standardised list of questions about the firm, fund, team, terms and operations.Sent by LPs and consultants, often in ILPA format [S320] REPORTED. Answered by product specialists with a central team (below).
RFP (request for proposal), called a tender in the UK and EUA formal competition: the client describes a mandate and invites several managers to bid.Typical of public pensions and DC schemes. Answered by the same machine, with more pricing and process detail INFERRED.
ODD (operational due diligence)A separate review of the back office: valuation, cash controls, compliance, cyber, service providers.Run by a specialist ODD team at the LP or consultant [S458] CONFIRMED. Answered by Finance, Operations, Compliance, Technology INFERRED.

Who answers at KKR: the CAPS team

KKR has a dedicated support function. Client and Product Services (CAPS) answers RFPs, RFIs (requests for information) and DDQs from Dublin and New York [S146] CONFIRMED. A Dublin product posting asks the analyst to "Work with the internal CAPS team to complete tenders/Due Diligence Questionnaires for potential new clients across credit while leading investor diligence processes" [S106] CONFIRMED. The 2027 Frankfurt KCS intern will "Coordinate responses to client due diligence requests/RFPs, including underlying portfolio statistics, marketing materials and presentation decks" and work with "strategies teams, client services, the RFP team" [S141] CONFIRMED. So an intern in London is likely to sit between three groups: the relationship manager who owns the client, the product team who owns the content, and CAPS who owns the process INFERRED.

The industry template: ILPA DDQ 2.0

The Institutional Limited Partners Association (ILPA), the LP trade body, publishes a standard DDQ. Version 2.0 was updated in November 2021, runs to 58 pages, and has 20 numbered sections and 8 appendices [S444] CONFIRMED. By my count it holds roughly 320 numbered question lines (estimate, as of the November 2021 text) [S444] INFERRED. The 2021 update added sections on succession planning, co-investment, GP-led secondaries, credit facilities and cybersecurity; the ESG section follows the PRI's LP questionnaire [S320] REPORTED. Figure 5 lays out all twenty sections and who inside a firm would typically own each answer.

Read the owner labels again. A single DDQ needs Legal, Finance, Compliance, Technology, HR, the deal team and KCM. That matches the London analyst posting almost word for word: "Collaborating with various internal divisions globally to answer client requests (Product teams, Investment teams, Operations, Finance, Legal, Compliance, Risk)" [S101] CONFIRMED.

Operational due diligence: what it covers

Cambridge Associates, a large investment consultant, describes the ODD view of valuation precisely: it is "not to audit or underwrite specific asset valuations, but rather to assess a manager's controls, policy, oversight, and governance" [S458] CONFIRMED. It stresses open-end and evergreen funds, "where transactions are based on the fund's NAV", because "Fee calculations rely on accurate valuations"; oversight should come from "a valuation committee, fund auditors, and the board" [S458] CONFIRMED.

ODD areaThe question behind itKKR evidence a junior might point to
Valuation governanceWho sets the marks, who checks them, how often?K-PEC: the Manager "determines the NAV of our Shares monthly"; an Administrator computes per-share NAV; all portfolio companies are Level III [S420] CONFIRMED.
Fund administration and cash controlsCan money move without two people approving?Not public; answered by Finance and Operations [Gap].
Compliance and conflictsHow are allocation and conflicts handled?K-PEC's allocation tiers [S420]; a Global Conflicts and Compliance Committee [S121] CONFIRMED.
Business continuity and cyberWhat happens if systems fail or are attacked?ILPA section 18 and Appendix D [S444]; KKR's answers are not public [Gap].
Regulatory historyAny enforcement, and what changed after?SEC broken-deal case 2015 [S20]; off-channel sweep 2025 [S29]. Use only approved wording INFERRED.

Regulators look at the same thing. The FCA's March 2025 review of private market valuation practices covered 36 firms and found weaknesses in valuation governance records, conflicts identification, independence and ad hoc revaluations [S310] REPORTED.

Side letters, MFN elections and the 2025 ILPA reporting templates@@l5-terms@@

Side letters

All investors in a fund sign the same Limited Partnership Agreement (LPA). Individual LPs often negotiate extra terms in a private side agreement called a side letter: fee breaks, reporting rights, ESG commitments, co-investment rights, or "excuse rights" that let an LP skip deals it cannot legally hold [Inferred, from WS4 standard terms].

The most-favoured-nation (MFN) clause

The law firm CMS defines it: an MFN "typically entitles an investor to elect the benefit of rights contained in other investors' side letters and potentially also the right to view the other investors' side letters" [S456] CONFIRMED. Three mechanics make MFNs technical:

  • Tiering. An LP can usually only elect terms given to LPs with equal or smaller commitments [S457] REPORTED.
  • Carve-outs. Standard exclusions cover legal, regulatory and tax-status terms, LPAC (advisory committee) seats, co-investment rights and size-based fee breaks [S457] REPORTED. Sponsors use carve-outs to "afford larger, first close or repeat investors more favourable rights" [S456] CONFIRMED.
  • The election window. Sponsors "require MFN elections to be submitted within a certain period following closing" and "Late MFN election submissions are at risk of being rejected" [S456] CONFIRMED.

The procedure in words. (1) After the final close, the sponsor sends each MFN holder a compendium of side-letter terms it is entitled to see. (2) The client's counsel filters by tier: keep only terms granted to LPs at or below $250m. (3) Strike carve-outs: size-based fee breaks, LPAC seats, co-investment rights, legal, regulatory and tax-status terms. (4) Choose what to elect and return the election form before the deadline. (5) The sponsor updates its obligations register so that, for example, the ESG pack actually goes out every quarter [S456, S457; steps 1 and 5 Inferred]. CMS notes the process adds "financial, administrative and disclosure burdens on sponsors, particularly in open-ended products" [S456] CONFIRMED: an evergreen fund closes every month, so the compendium never stops growing INFERRED.

The 2025 ILPA templates: what clients will expect in reports

TemplateWhat it addsWhen it applies
Reporting Template (released Jan 2025)A gross-to-net management fee reconciliation (rebates, waivers, offsets); granular partnership expenses, including chargebacks to the GP; capitalised transaction fees; "granular accounting of incentive allocation amounts" [S445] CONFIRMED.Funds "still in their investment period during Q1 2026" or starting on or after 1 January 2026 [S445]; first delivery covers Q1 2026 [S318] CONFIRMED.
Performance Template (released Jan 2025)Two methods, "granular" (itemised cash flows) and "gross-up". Standardises IRR and TVPI/MOIC, gross and net, "with and without the impact of fund-level subscription facilities" [S445] CONFIRMED.Funds starting on or after 1 January 2026; first delivery four full fiscal quarters later [S445] CONFIRMED.
Capital Call and Distribution TemplateRefreshed later in 2025 [S446] CONFIRMED.Portfolio Company Template refresh slated for January 2027 [S446].

Notice the link back to the two fee pipes. The Reporting Template's "gross-to-net management fee reconciliation (rebates, waivers, offsets)" is exactly where the 100% fee offset from Figure 2 becomes visible to each LP, line by line [S445, S425] INFERRED.

How a junior drafts a DDQ answer@@l5-drafting@@

A DDQ answer is not written from memory. It is assembled from approved sources, checked by the person who owns the fact, reviewed by Compliance, and stored so that the next answer starts from it. Figure 7 is the life cycle of one answer. The method is standard practice, reconstructed INFERRED; the fact that juniors do this work with CAPS and the product teams is sourced [S106, S141].

Don't do this

Copy a number from last year's DDQ without checking its date. Round a performance figure "to look cleaner". Answer a question about another client's terms. Each turns a DDQ into a misstatement INFERRED.

What you will learn

The structure of the industry-standard DDQ section by section; the difference between investment diligence, an RFP and ODD; how to route a question to the right internal owner; how to run a tiered MFN election; what the 2025 ILPA templates require; and how to draft an answer from a source log through sign-off.

CAPS in Dublin and New York [S146]; the Frankfurt intern's "due diligence requests/RFPs" duty [S141]; product specialists who are "leading investor diligence processes" [S106] CONFIRMED. Evergreen products such as K-PEC face the toughest ODD questions on monthly NAV [S420, S458].

Only at KKR

Any large manager answers DDQs. At KKR the same machine must cover drawdown flagships, evergreen K-Series vehicles, insurance sidecars and co-investment, each with different legal forms (an LLC "conglomerate", a BDC, a registered fund) [S420, S422, S424] CONFIRMED. So one intern sees several versions of section 12 (fund terms) and section 15 (valuation) side by side INFERRED.

Say it in one line

Distribution rules a KCS junior applies every day@@l5-rules@@

Every deck, email and DDQ is a marketing communication in the eyes of some regulator. Whether it may be sent depends on three things: who the person is, where they are, and what the product is. KKR's own 10-K says that outside the US it relies on "private placement exemptions and marketing registrations, for example under the AIFMD in Europe, the Financial Services and Markets Act 2000 ... and the Alternative Investment Fund Managers Regulations 2013 ... in the United Kingdom" [S70] CONFIRMED. This section turns that sentence into the checks a junior runs.

The rulebook in one table

RuleWhat it does, in plain EnglishKey facts and dates
Professional vs retailRegulators split clients into professional (institutions, large firms) and retail (individuals, unless they qualify otherwise). Rules for retail are far stricter. The exact tests are Compliance's call INFERRED.KKR's wealth site states its materials are "only intended and directed to Institutional, Professional or Accredited Investors" [S83] CONFIRMED.
AIFMD and the passportThe EU law for managers of alternative funds. An EU manager can market EU funds to professional investors across the EU with a passport. Non-EU managers use each country's national private placement regime (NPPR), under Article 42.Directive 2011/61/EU; passport live 22 Jul 2013 [S300] CONFIRMED. KKR's EU AIFM is authorised by the Central Bank of Ireland [S70] CONFIRMED.
AIFMD IIUpdate covering loan origination, liquidity tools, delegation and tighter NPPR conditions.Directive (EU) 2024/927, applies 16 Apr 2026 [S294, S296]; NPPR funds must not be in EU AML high-risk or tax non-cooperative jurisdictions [S294] REPORTED; open-ended AIFs must pick at least two liquidity tools [S449] CONFIRMED.
Pre-marketingTesting interest before a fund is set up or notified, without an offer.An EU AIFM must notify its home regulator within two weeks of starting; any subscription by professional investors within 18 months counts as marketing [S299] CONFIRMED.
Reverse solicitationThe investor approaches the manager entirely on its own initiative, so no marketing occurred.The 18-month rule effectively blocks relying on it after pre-marketing [S299] CONFIRMED. A junior should never assume it applies INFERRED.
ELTIF 2.0EU long-term fund label open to retail investors.Regulation (EU) 2023/606 applies from 10 Jan 2024 [S301, S302]; RTS 2024/2759 on redemptions [S447] CONFIRMED; 189 new ELTIFs since 2024, about €34bn at end-2025 [S302] REPORTED. No KKR ELTIF found [Gap].
UK LTAFUK open-ended long-term fund.Rules in force 15 Nov 2021 [S306]; units recategorised as Restricted Mass Market Investments from 3 Jul 2023 [S305] CONFIRMED; dealing no more than monthly, 90+ days' notice [S451] REPORTED; £7.5bn AUM (May 2026) [S452] REPORTED. No KKR LTAF found [Gap].
UK financial promotionsPromotions to individuals must be approved or fall within an exemption.Certified high-net-worth thresholds: £100,000 income or £250,000 net assets, restored 27 Mar 2024 [S307] CONFIRMED.
Consumer DutyFirms must deliver good outcomes for retail customers, including fair value.Open products from 31 Jul 2023, closed products from 31 Jul 2024 [S308] CONFIRMED. Managers "may still fall in scope where they sit in a distribution chain to retail customers" [S452] REPORTED.
MiFID II product governanceManufacturers and distributors define a target market for each product and check the client fits it.MiFID II applied from 3 Jan 2018 [Inferred, date not re-verified]. KKR's 10-K: these rules cover "marketing, investor suitability assessments, and conflicts of interest management" [S70] CONFIRMED.
US: Reg D and accredited investorsPrivate offerings in the US are limited to qualifying investors.Rule 506(b): no general solicitation; 506(c): solicitation allowed if all buyers are verified accredited [S314]. Accredited: income over $200,000 ($300,000 joint) or net worth over $1m excluding home [S314] CONFIRMED. K-PEC sells under Reg D and Reg S [S420] CONFIRMED.
US Marketing RuleSEC rule 206(4)-1 for adviser advertisements, including performance.Adopted 22 Dec 2020; compliance date 4 Nov 2022 [S313] CONFIRMED.
Source uncertain

The detailed performance rules inside the Marketing Rule (for example, how gross returns must be accompanied by net returns) were not read in our research files. Treat any specific rule here as something to confirm with Compliance INFERRED. What is sourced is the industry direction: the ILPA Performance Template standardises IRR and TVPI "gross and net" and "with and without the impact of fund-level subscription facilities" [S445] CONFIRMED. Why it matters, using the illustrative fund from the co-investment section: gross 2.0x, net 1.74x. Showing 2.0x alone to an investor who will receive 1.74x overstates the outcome by 26 cents per dollar invested [Inferred; numbers illustrative].

The daily decision: "can I send this deck to this person?"

Figure 8 puts the rulebook into a single tree. It is a learning aid, not legal advice: the real answer always comes from KKR's Compliance team. It shows the order of questions, which is the skill.

Where the tree bites at KKR

  • K-PRIME to a European private bank. K-PRIME is the Luxembourg twin of K-PEC with "similar objectives and strategies" [S111] CONFIRMED; its product development is run from London by Matt Yates [S133] CONFIRMED. Its own documents describe a Part II UCI under Luxembourg's 2010 law, supervised by the CSSF [S429] REPORTED. A deck for a Swiss or German private bank runs down the EU column of Figure 8; the bank's own clients bring in MiFID II target market questions INFERRED.
  • A UK wealth manager. KKR announced that Quilter Cheviot would offer KKR's evergreen private equity strategy from January 2026 [S177] CONFIRMED. Quilter is the distributor; Consumer Duty means KKR must give it what it needs to show fair value [S452] [Reported; application Inferred].
  • A US adviser. Nuttall: "K Series is built to be able to go to the accredited investor in the U.S., which is $1 million and up in net worth" [S76] REPORTED. The SEC's late-September 2026 proposals would add routes such as a FINRA accredited-investor exam and the CFA charter [S275] REPORTED, which would change US-1 in the tree.
  • The UK regime itself is moving. FCA CP26/28 (July 2026) proposes three tiers of UK AIFM by NAV and keeps NPPR with limited changes; comments close 14 October 2026 and new rules are expected in force in 2028 [S303] CONFIRMED.
What you will learn

To classify a recipient (professional or retail, US or UK or EEA), to know which permission a product has in each place, and to run the send decision in the right order: approval, location, eligibility, performance, channel, record.

Where you meet it at KKR

KKR's UK entities are FCA-authorised; its EU AIFM is Central Bank of Ireland-authorised [S70] CONFIRMED. KCS is in London, Dublin, Zurich, Paris, Stockholm, Frankfurt and the Gulf [S80] CONFIRMED, so one EMEA team sends into many regimes at once.

Only at KKR

KKR sells the same strategy through drawdown funds for institutions, evergreen vehicles under US Reg D and Reg S [S420], a Luxembourg vehicle for Europe [S429], and a third party's US interval funds [S427]. A London junior therefore sees one investment idea pass through several legal wrappers and several rulebooks. That breadth is the learning, inferred from the product range INFERRED.

The tools and the record@@l5-tools@@

KCS work leaves a trail: every meeting, every document sent, every onboarding step. The tools exist to make that trail complete and searchable. What is confirmed about KKR's toolset is short, so this section separates sourced facts from method.

What is confirmed: Salesforce is named in a KKR wealth posting, which asks for "Proficiency in CRM systems (Salesforce)" and "meeting/call notes in CRM" [S103] CONFIRMED; the Singapore intern maintains "CRM data" [S107] CONFIRMED; the London analyst is "Partnering with clients to help with KYC & investment onboarding" and "Managing a variety of reporting requirements for investors" [S101] CONFIRMED. KKR notes that "Financial crime laws may limit our investment and capital raising activities" [S70] CONFIRMED, which is why KYC sits on the critical path to a closing. What is not public: the data room vendor, any use of DealCloud, and the internal answer library's form [Gap].

AI: what is public, and what is not

  • The internship text. KKR's internship wording says interns are "encouraged to leverage KKR's approved AI tools" (quoted in the candidate brief).
  • The London analyst posting. It asks for "Strong working knowledge of AI-enabled productivity tools, with the ability to use generative AI effectively and responsibly" [S101] CONFIRMED.
  • The firm's platform. KKR's Technology team advertised an "AI Platform Lead" in New York to "Define, build, and scale a centralized AI platform to enable a new generation of intelligent, business-facing applications" and to "Drive the adoption of Generative AI, NLP, and ML across the firm" (listing removed 23 Feb 2026) [S443] CONFIRMED. A job advert shows intent, not deployment.
  • What is not public. I found no public KKR statement on how KCS itself uses AI. DDQ drafting, RFP search, meeting notes and CRM summaries are plausible uses but unconfirmed [S443] [Gap].
From the internship advert

KKR's 2027 London internship text says: "Interns are encouraged to leverage KKR's approved AI tools to accelerate their work, deepen their learning, and contribute fresh perspectives on how emerging technology can enhance the way we operate" [Advert]. The London analyst posting also asks for generative AI skills [S140]. How KCS uses those tools day to day is not public.
